From the 1845 law to midnight votes, 15 facts about how Election Day works

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The reason Americans vote on a Tuesday goes back to an 1845 law and a farm calendar, and 15 slides trace the history through ballots, amendments and traditions

The U.S. picks its presidents on a schedule designed for a very different country. When Congress settled on a single national voting day in 1845, most Americans lived in rural areas and worked in agriculture. Many had to reach a county seat to cast a ballot, and that trip could take a day or more. The calendar lawmakers chose reflected long trips, harvests and Sunday worship — not office jobs, commutes or mail-in ballots.

That 1845 law fixed the date for choosing presidential electors as the Tuesday after the first Monday in November. It said nothing about why. The usual reasons come from historical accounts, not from the statute's text. They include a harvest finished by November, roads still passable before winter and a Sunday kept free of travel. Other stories, such as Wednesday being market day, get repeated so often that they sound settled. They are commonly cited explanations, not documented ones.

Knowing why Americans vote on a Tuesday also explains how the rest of the system grew around that date. Before 1845, states set their own dates within a window Congress allowed, and voting stretched across weeks. In 1872, Congress extended the same November date to House elections. Senate races joined after the 17th Amendment gave voters, not state legislatures, the power to elect senators. The date stayed put while the rules about who could vote, and how, changed around it.

The secret ballot replaced the often public practice of voting by voice or with party-printed tickets. Constitutional amendments protected women's right to vote, banned poll taxes in federal elections and set the voting age at 18. Congress passed the Voting Rights Act in 1965. Each shift happened on top of a calendar first drawn for a farming economy.

The first slides cover the calendar itself: the law, the month, the weekday and the specific phrasing that keeps Election Day from ever landing on Nov. 1. The next group traces how elections for Congress joined the same date and how access to the ballot widened. The last group looks at the traditions and rules that surround the day now. Those include a New Hampshire town's midnight vote and the fact that Election Day is not a federal holiday. They also compare how other democracies schedule elections and trace the weeks between the vote and the inauguration.

Americans vote on a Tuesday because of a federal law Congress passed in 1845. It set one national day for choosing presidential electors: the Tuesday after the first Monday in November. That rule still fixes the date of presidential elections.

The law covered presidential elections only. It did not set the timing of congressional races, which stayed under state control for decades afterward. Voters were also not choosing a president directly. They were choosing the electors who would cast the formal votes for president on behalf of each state.

Congress had clear authority to act. Article II of the Constitution lets Congress determine the time of choosing electors and the day on which they give their votes. It also requires that the electors' voting day be the same throughout the nation. The 1845 statute used that power to fix a single Election Day for the whole country.

President John Tyler signed the measure in January 1845. The 1844 race had already been decided under the older arrangement. The new rule first applied four years later. The 1848 contest was the first presidential election held under the single-day system. Zachary Taylor, the Whig candidate, won it.

The law also included a fallback provision. It allowed a state that failed to make a choice on Election Day to appoint its electors on a later day. The state legislature could direct how that later appointment happened. That clause stayed on the books for more than 170 years. Congress narrowed it in the Electoral Count Reform Act of 2022. The newer law permits a state to extend its voting period only for extraordinary and catastrophic events.

The 1845 law set the timing of the choice, not its method. Each state legislature still decided how its electors would be appointed. South Carolina's legislature kept picking the state's electors itself, rather than holding a popular vote, until after the Civil War. The Election Day rule now sits in Title 3 of the U.S. Code, the part of federal law that governs presidential elections.

Election Day was built around the working life of the American farmer. According to the standard historical account, when Congress set a national voting date in 1845, the calendar had to suit a country where most voters lived and worked on farms.

Distance was the central problem. For many rural voters, casting a ballot meant traveling to the county seat. That trip could take a day or more.

Travel moved at the pace of a horse. In the 1840s, a voter generally made the journey on horseback, by wagon or on foot. Most roads were unpaved. Rain could turn those dirt roads to mud and lengthen the trip. Railroads were still new and covered only a small part of the country. Few farm families could count on a train to reach the polls.

A trip of that length turned voting into an errand measured in days, not hours. A voter had to leave home, reach the polling place, cast a ballot and then make the return journey.

For a farm family, each of those days carried a cost. Time on the road was time away from crops and livestock. In the standard account, a workable date had to leave room for the journey in both directions, not only for the moment at the ballot box.

That logic belonged to the electorate of the 1840s. The U.S. later became a mostly urban country. The 1920 census was the first to count more Americans living in urban areas than in rural ones. For that count, the Census Bureau treated places with 2,500 or more residents as urban. That crossover arrived 75 years after the 1845 law.

The federal voting date did not move with that shift. Election Day still falls on the schedule Congress fixed in 1845, when reaching the polls could mean a day or more on the road.

Election Day landed on Tuesday largely by elimination. Sunday was reserved for worship, and a Monday vote would have forced many voters to travel on Sunday.

The Sunday problem was the simplest to see. In the mid-1800s, Sunday was a day of worship for much of the country. Holding an election on it would have put civic duty in direct competition with church. The national date Congress set in 1845 kept voting off the Sabbath.

Monday created a second-order problem. Rural voters who lived far from the polls needed travel time before they could cast a ballot. A Monday election would have meant leaving home on Sunday. That ran into the same objection that ruled out Sunday voting itself. Tuesday gave those voters a full day to make the trip after worship.

Wednesday is where the history gets less certain. A frequently repeated account holds that Wednesday was market day in many places, when farmers brought goods to town to sell. Under that account, a Wednesday election would have cut into a working day that rural families depended on.

That market-day explanation is a commonly cited reason, not documented intent. The 1845 statute fixes the date without recording a rationale for any weekday. The reasons attached to it today are reconstructions built from the conditions of the period. They are not language written into the law.

Taken together, the three constraints point toward a narrow window. A voter could attend worship on Sunday, travel on Monday and cast a ballot on Tuesday. Under the market-day account, that voter could also be back for Wednesday's trading. The commonly repeated explanation does not address Thursday, Friday or Saturday, and the law itself is silent on them too.

The result has outlasted the farm economy that shaped it. The same Tuesday rule still governs federal elections, even though few Americans now farm or plan their week around a town market.

Election Day falls in November because the month offered a workable window in a farming country. The standard historical explanation holds that the harvest was largely finished by then, while winter had not yet closed the roads.

That explanation comes from historical accounts, not from the law. The 1845 statute that created a single national date for choosing presidential electors names the month without explaining it.

The farm side of the reasoning follows the agricultural year. Spring meant planting. Summer and early autumn meant tending and gathering crops. A vote during those months would have pulled people away from the fields at the busiest time. Much of that work was done by November.

The weather side concerns travel. Many voters went to the polls by horse, by wagon or on foot over unpaved roads. Snow, ice and mud could make those routes hard or impossible to use later in the year. An early November vote came before the worst of winter across much of the country.

Those conditions were not the same everywhere. Winter arrived far earlier in New England than along the Gulf Coast. The 1845 law still set one national date for every state.

The November timing also fit the federal schedule already in place. A 1792 law had let states choose electors during a window that closed before the electors met in December. The 1845 date fell inside that window, so the electors' December meeting did not have to move.

The first presidential election held on the uniform date took place on Nov. 7, 1848. Federal law still sets the presidential vote in November, in Title 3 of the U.S. Code. That vote comes every fourth year, in years divisible by four, such as 2024 and 2028. The date has outlasted the farm economy that is said to have shaped it.

The phrase "the Tuesday after the first Monday in November" fixes federal Election Day to a date between Nov. 2 and Nov. 8. That wording means the vote can never land on Nov. 1, even in years when the month begins on a Tuesday.

The rule works in two steps. Find the first Monday of November, then move forward one day. A simpler instruction, such as "the first Tuesday in November," would put the election on Nov. 1 whenever the month opens on a Tuesday. The extra step removes that possibility.

The 2016 presidential election shows the formula at work. Nov. 1, 2016, fell on a Tuesday, so the first Monday of that month was Nov. 7. Voters went to the polls the next day, Nov. 8. That is the latest date the formula allows.

The other end of the range appears when November starts on a Monday. In 2004, Nov. 1 was a Monday, which made Nov. 2 Election Day. The 2020 election, held on Nov. 3, landed near the start of the window.

The 1845 law that created the formula does not say why lawmakers wrote it this way. Two reasons for keeping the vote off Nov. 1 are commonly cited. Both are later explanations, not reasons recorded in the statute.

The first involves All Saints' Day. The Christian feast falls on Nov. 1. For Roman Catholics, it is a holy day of obligation, when attendance at Mass is expected. The explanation holds that lawmakers wanted to avoid scheduling a national vote on a major religious observance.

The second involves commerce. Merchants are often said to have used the first of the month to close out the previous month's accounts. Under this account, a Nov. 1 vote would have collided with a day many business owners set aside for bookkeeping.

The result is a window of seven possible dates. The formula depends only on the calendar, so any future Election Day can be worked out years ahead. The 2028 presidential election, for example, falls on Nov. 7.

Before 1845, there was no single Election Day for president. Each state picked its own date for choosing presidential electors, as long as it fell inside a window Congress set in 1792.

That 1792 federal law gave states a 34-day window. Electors could be appointed at any point during the 34 days before the first Wednesday in December, the day the electors then met to vote. A state could hold its contest early in that span or late in it.

The Constitution made this arrangement possible. Article II gives Congress the power to set the time for choosing electors and the day on which electors cast their votes. It requires that the electors' voting day be the same throughout the U.S. It sets no such uniformity rule for choosing the electors themselves. Until 1845, Congress left that timing to the states within the federal window.

Article II also leaves the method of appointing electors to each state legislature. In the early decades, many legislatures chose electors themselves instead of holding a popular vote. By the 1830s, South Carolina was the only state still doing so. Its legislature kept picking the state's electors through the 1860 election. Where a popular vote did happen, the state set its own date for it.

The practical result was a presidential election spread across weeks. Voters in one state could go to the polls well before voters in another. The national contest unfolded in pieces, with each state on its own schedule.

That staggered calendar created a timing problem. Results from states that voted early could become known before other states had voted at all. Voters in later states could cast ballots after some of those outcomes were already known.

That problem is a commonly cited reason for a single national day. Congress settled the question in 1845. It replaced the open window with one fixed date for choosing presidential electors in every state.

Congress put elections for the U.S. House of Representatives on the Tuesday after the first Monday in November through a law passed in 1872. That measure gave House races the same national date already used for choosing presidential electors.

The 1872 law arrived 27 years after the 1845 statute that first created a single national Election Day. The earlier law covered presidential elections only. It set no date for congressional contests.

Before 1872, each state picked its own date for House elections. Some states held those races on the same day as presidential voting. Others held them on different days. The country had no single date for choosing its representatives.

The Constitution's Elections Clause gave Congress the authority to make the change. The clause leaves the times, places and manner of congressional elections to state legislatures. It also lets Congress make or alter those rules. The 1872 law used that federal power to set one date for House races across the country.

After 1872, House elections shared the November date with presidential voting. The Constitution puts every House seat up for election every two years. As a result, the law placed a federal contest on the November ballot in years without a presidential race, too.

U.S. Senate seats were not part of this change. State legislatures still chose senators at the time, so there was no popular Senate vote to schedule.

Voters have elected U.S. senators directly only since 1913, when the 17th Amendment was ratified. Before that, state legislatures chose senators. The change moved Senate races onto the same national November date that voters already used for other federal offices.

The original Constitution set up the Senate differently from the House. Article I, Section 3 gave each state two senators. It also said they would be chosen by the state's legislature for six-year terms. House members, by contrast, were elected by the people from the start. The framers designed the two chambers to answer to different constituencies.

Under that system, a voter's influence over the Senate was indirect. Citizens elected state lawmakers, and those lawmakers then picked the senators. A Senate seat could hinge on which party controlled a state legislature rather than on a statewide vote.

The 17th Amendment rewrote that arrangement. Its text provides that the Senate is made up of two senators from each state, elected by the people of that state, for six years. It also requires voters in Senate elections to meet the same qualifications as voters for the most numerous branch of their state legislature. That wording tied eligibility for Senate voting to rules each state already used.

The amendment also addressed vacancies. It directs a state's governor to call an election to fill an open seat. A state legislature may empower the governor to make a temporary appointment until voters fill the seat.

Once senators were chosen by popular vote, their races joined the federal ballot on the Tuesday after the first Monday in November. Presidential, House and Senate contests now share that date.

The six-year term did not change. Senate terms are staggered. About one-third of the seats come up in each two-year election cycle. As a result, each regular November general election includes Senate races in only some states. Every House seat, by contrast, is on the ballot each time.

The secret ballot let Americans mark a government-printed ballot in private, replacing a system in which many votes were cast in public. Massachusetts adopted the approach statewide in 1888, and other states followed over the next several years.

Before that shift, casting a vote was often a visible act. In some places, voters announced their choices aloud at the polling place. Elsewhere, they handed in tickets printed and distributed by political parties rather than by election officials.

A party ticket usually listed only that party's candidates. A voter could pick one up from a party worker, carry it to the polls and drop it in the ballot box. The paper itself could show anyone watching which side a voter supported.

That visibility had consequences. An employer, a landlord or a party operative standing near the polls could see how a person voted. Someone paying for votes could confirm that the purchase had been delivered. Voters who depended on others for work or housing had reason to think twice before breaking with them in public.

The reform became known as the Australian ballot. It took that name because the colony of Victoria, in Australia, introduced it in 1856, decades before it reached the U.S. Under the system, election officials, not parties, printed the ballots. Every qualifying candidate appeared on the same sheet. Voters marked their choices out of view of others.

Private marking meant a vote buyer could no longer check what a voter had done. A single official ballot listing all candidates made it easier to choose people from different parties in the same election. Government printing also meant the state now had to decide whose names appeared on the ballot. That pushed lawmakers to write rules for how candidates qualified.

The ballot most Americans fill out on the Tuesday after the first Monday in November descends from this late-19th-century design. The private voting booth, the uniform official ballot and the expectation that no one else sees a voter's choices all trace back to it.

The 19th Amendment, ratified in 1920, bars the federal government and the states from denying the vote on account of sex. The presidential election of Nov. 2, 1920 was the first in which women across the country could vote with that right protected by the Constitution.

The amendment's core text is a single sentence. It says the right of citizens of the U.S. to vote "shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or by any State on account of sex." A second section gives Congress the power to enforce that guarantee through legislation.

The proposal took more than four decades to clear Congress. Sen. Aaron Sargent of California introduced the language in 1878. It became known as the Susan B. Anthony Amendment. The House approved it on May 21, 1919. The Senate followed on June 4, 1919, sending it to the states.

Ratification required approval from three-fourths of the 48 states then in the union. Tennessee became the 36th state to ratify on Aug. 18, 1920. That vote supplied the final approval needed. Secretary of State Bainbridge Colby certified the amendment on Aug. 26, 1920.

The November vote that followed kept the schedule Congress had set in 1845. Nov. 2, 1920 was the Tuesday after the first Monday of the month. Republican Warren G. Harding defeated Democrat James M. Cox in that race.

The push for a national guarantee began long before 1920. The Seneca Falls Convention in New York issued a call for women's voting rights in 1848. Wyoming's territorial legislature granted women the vote in 1869. Several Western states extended full voting rights to women before the amendment took effect. The 19th Amendment replaced that state-by-state patchwork with a constitutional standard.

The amendment did not put a ballot in every woman's hand in practice. Many Black women in Southern states stayed blocked by the same discriminatory registration rules used against Black men. Many Native American women could not vote because the U.S. did not yet recognize them as citizens. Congress passed the Indian Citizenship Act in 1924. Some states still restricted Native voting for years afterward.

Three changes between 1964 and 1971 widened who could cast a ballot on Election Day. The 24th Amendment, ratified in 1964, banned poll taxes in federal elections. The Voting Rights Act became law in 1965. The 26th Amendment, ratified in 1971, set the voting age at 18.

A poll tax was a fee charged as a condition of voting. By the 1960s it survived mainly in Southern states. There it had long served to keep Black voters and poor white voters away from the polls. The 24th Amendment covered only federal races — presidential, Senate and House contests. Its text left state and local elections untouched.

That gap closed two years later. In Harper v. Virginia Board of Elections, decided in 1966, the Supreme Court ruled that poll taxes in state elections violated the Equal Protection Clause of the 14th Amendment. The amendment and the ruling, taken together, ended the practice.

The Voting Rights Act reached further than the poll tax ban. President Lyndon B. Johnson signed it on Aug. 6, 1965. That came months after state troopers attacked civil rights marchers on the Edmund Pettus Bridge in Selma, Alabama. The law targeted tools such as literacy tests, which local officials had used to turn away Black applicants. It also allowed federal examiners to register voters in some places.

One of its central provisions, known as preclearance, required jurisdictions covered by the law to get federal approval before changing their voting rules. In Shelby County v. Holder, decided in 2013, the Supreme Court struck down the formula that determined which jurisdictions were covered. Without a valid formula, the preclearance requirement no longer applies to the jurisdictions it once covered. Other parts of the act remain in force.

The 26th Amendment lowered the minimum voting age to 18 in federal, state and local elections. Before it, most states set the minimum at 21. The push gained force during the Vietnam War. Men could be drafted at 18, but in most states they could not vote. Supporters rallied around the slogan "old enough to fight, old enough to vote."

Congress had first tried to lower the age by statute. In Oregon v. Mitchell, decided in 1970, the Supreme Court ruled that Congress could set an 18-year-old voting age for federal elections but not for state ones. That split would have forced many states to run separate voting systems for different races. A constitutional amendment resolved the problem, and the states ratified it in 1971.

Dixville Notch, a small town in New Hampshire, casts its Election Day ballots at midnight, in the first minutes of the voting day. The town has held this midnight vote since 1960.

Federal Election Day falls on a Tuesday. In Dixville Notch, voting begins as soon as Monday night turns into Tuesday morning. Most polling places in the U.S. open hours later, in the morning. That timing puts the town's ballots in before most of the country has started voting.

The tradition started in a presidential election year. The town has repeated the midnight vote in the decades since. Its ballots are now among the first cast on Election Day in the U.S.

The early start changes only when residents vote, not the rules they vote under. Dixville Notch uses the same national date as every other community. Its ballots count toward New Hampshire's results the same way any other town's do.

This slide falls short of the 300-word minimum at about 150 words. The verified material for Dixville Notch, New Hampshire covers only the midnight voting tradition and its 1960 start, and vote tallies are excluded by the brief, so no further facts could be added without padding or invention.

Election Day is a regular working day in the U.S. Federal law fixes the date for federal elections, but no federal law makes that Tuesday a federal holiday.

That leaves time off to the states. Some states have their own provisions that give workers time off to vote. Others treat Election Day as a state holiday. The rules vary, so what a voter can expect depends on where they live and work.

The date and the holiday question sit in different parts of federal law. The 1845 law and the statutes that followed it set when federal elections take place. Federal holidays are listed separately, in Title 5, Section 6103, of the U.S. Code, and Election Day is not among them.

The list sets paid days off for federal employees. Federal law does not require private employers to close on any of those days. Federal offices stay open on Election Day, and it runs like any other Tuesday on the federal calendar.

One federal holiday sits close by. Veterans Day falls on Nov. 11. Federal Election Day can land no later than Nov. 8, so the two never coincide. Veterans Day arrives three to nine days after the vote.

For voters, working-day status shapes the time they have. People with fixed shifts who vote in person that day must go before work, after work or during a break, unless they get time off. Many states also offer early in-person voting or mail ballots, which let people vote without a Tuesday trip to the polls. Polling hours, which states set, decide how much room the day itself leaves.

The result is a split system. The date is national and fixed by federal law. Any time away from work to use it comes from state law, an employer's own policy or a voter's own schedule.

Germany holds its federal elections on Sundays, while U.K. general elections have traditionally fallen on Thursdays. Many other countries also schedule national votes on weekends. The American Tuesday is one answer among several to the question of when a country should vote.

Germany's Sunday schedule moves the federal vote outside the Monday-to-Friday workweek. A voter on a standard weekday schedule does not have to arrange time off, leave early or line up before a shift to take part.

The U.K. custom sits closer to the American model. Thursday, like Tuesday, falls in the middle of the working week. Voters with weekday jobs in either country have to fit a trip to the polling place around their hours on the job.

Weekend voting in other countries follows the same reasoning as Germany's Sunday elections. For people who work Monday through Friday, a Saturday or Sunday vote falls on a day off. The trade-off runs the other way for people whose jobs include weekend shifts. They face the same scheduling problem a weekday voter faces in the U.S. or U.K.

Set side by side, these approaches show that the day of the week is a choice each country makes, not a fixed feature of national elections. Germany and many other countries put the vote on a weekend. The U.K. and the U.S. keep it on a weekday.

Election Day does not install a president. Voters choose electors in November, those electors cast the formal votes in December, and the new term begins at noon on Jan. 20 under the 20th Amendment, ratified in 1933.

The electors meet in their own states rather than in one national gathering. The Constitution requires that arrangement. Under the 12th Amendment, each state's electors cast separate ballots for president and vice president. The results are sealed and sent to Washington, D.C.

Electors are chosen through the popular vote in each state. Their December ballots are the formal step between November's results and the official count in Congress.

Congress then counts the electoral votes in a joint session in January. The sitting vice president, acting as president of the Senate, opens the certificates. A candidate needs a majority of the 538 electoral votes, which is 270, to win outright. If no one reaches that mark, the House of Representatives chooses the president. In that case, each state delegation casts one vote.

For most of the country's history, the wait was far longer. Presidents took office on March 4, a date tied to the start of the federal government under the Constitution in 1789. That left about four months between the November vote and the transfer of power.

The long gap had real consequences. After Abraham Lincoln won in November 1860, seven Southern states seceded before he took office in March 1861. James Buchanan remained president through that winter.

The 20th Amendment cut the presidential wait by roughly six weeks. It also moved the start of new congressional terms to Jan. 3. As a result, a newly elected Congress is seated before it counts the electoral votes. The measure is often called the lame duck amendment. It shortened the period that outgoing officials, known as lame ducks, serve after an election.

Franklin D. Roosevelt was the last president first inaugurated on March 4, in 1933. His second inauguration, in 1937, was the first held on Jan. 20.

Disney plans several thousand layoffs, asks some managers to cut costs by up to 20%

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disney is planning to cut several thousand jobs, people familiar with the matter told CNBC.

Some managers at the entertainment giant have been asked to reduce costs by up to 20%, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly.

It marks the latest round of layoffs since CEO Josh D'Amaro took the reins earlier this year. The update comes after separate plans in April to cut as many as 1,000 employees, predominantly in the marketing department, and in September to cut about 300 employees, many in human resources and technology divisions.

Bloomberg was first to report the latest round of layoffs.

Company executives warned of the possibility of more reductions in its August earnings report.

"We remain highly focused on reducing costs across the enterprise to create incremental capacity to invest for growth and are evaluating a variety of levers," D'Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston wrote in an Aug. 5 letter to shareholders. "We are mid-stream in this work and will provide future updates on our progress."

Disney introduced plans in August for an early retirement program for some employees.

"This is one of several actions we're taking to reshape our organization, including involuntary staff reductions that have already begun in some areas and will continue into next year," Chief People Officer Sonia Coleman wrote in a letter to employees on Aug. 24.

Disney had about 231,000 employees as of the close of its fiscal year 2025, which ended in September of that year, according to the company's annual financial report.

Disney is not the only entertainment company looking to reduce costs. Netflix said in a memo last month that it would lay off roughly 6% of its workforce. Skydance, the newly combined company of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery, is also expected to cut staff in an effort to realize $6 billion in synergies over three years.

Disney will report its fiscal fourth-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

England star Ollie Watkins' wife Ellie flees their new life in Saudi Arabia amid deadly missile attacks - just months after £50m move to the Middle East

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published: 09:41 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 12:23 EDT, 3 November 2026

England striker Ollie Watkins' wife Ellie is fleeing Saudi Arabia to seek 'normality' for their children amid deadly missile attacks in the Middle East.

Ollie and Ellie, 31 and 30, moved to Saudi capital Riyadh in the summer with their two children after a £51million transfer from Aston Villa to Al-Hilal. But the recent deadly re-escalation of the Houthi-Saudi Arabia conflict has sparked a move back home for the family - with no end date in sight.

The Watkins family have two young children - Amara, five, and three-year-old Marley. Ellie initially admitted she had broken down in tears over the move but later felt 'everything was coming together'. Ellie and the children are now fleeing.

'We've had a bit of a plot twist,' Ellie said in a post on TikTok today. 'I have come back to the UK for, hopefully, a little bit. I don't actually know when.

Major airports in Saudi Arabia are operating reduced services after a missile attack by Yemen's Houthi movement on Riyadh's airport killed 13 people. Two people were also killed after an attack at King Fahd International Airport in Dammam.

'There's just a little bit of conflict and things going on over in Saudi and we basically decided just to stay here until it calms down,' Ellie added.

Ollie Watkins and wife Ellie moved to Saudi Arabia following his £50m summer transfer (Source: Instagram/Ellie Watkins)

Ellie has now returned to the UK amid missile attacks in Saudi (Source: Instagram/Ellie Watkins)

'I felt like I needed to do that for the kids so there is some form of normality until we can get back out there and all be together.'

Husband Ollie is hoping for a call-up to the England squad this week and will continue to play in the Saudi Pro League while his family reside in the UK.

Saudi football experts have suggested the striker, who has five goals and five assists in eight Saudi Pro League appearances so far, earns 'a minimum of £20million a year' - tax-free.

Al-Hilal play Shabab AlAhli, of Dubai, in the Asian Champions League today.

Ollie and Ellie have been settling into a new home, redecorating and finding schools for their children in Riyadh. Those plans have now been derailed.

Meanwhile, football's 2027 Asia Cup has been delayed by almost a year amid the ongoing Middle East crisis. The Six Kings Slam tennis exhibition event has also been called off while Formula One is expected to move its end-of-season races.

Ellie had said moving to Saudi Arabia has 'been a lot' but 'everything's finally coming together' - posting photos of their new life together (Source: Instagram/Ellie Watkins)

The WAG shared a snap of their stylish living room as she added: 'Everything's finally coming together and we're so happy in our new home' (Source: Instagram/Ellie Watkins)

Less than two months before it was due to kick off, the Asia Cup was today postponed until December 2027.

It makes the tournament not only a logistical nightmare for the 24 participating teams but also raises serious safety concerns.

A statement from the Asian Football Confederation said: 'The Organising Committee for AFC Asian Cup approved rescheduling the flagship national team tournament considering the current exceptional circumstances arising from developments in the region, including substantial logistical complexities and travel disruptions.

'The new dates of the tournament (December 23 2027 to January 21 2028) will be presented to FIFA for approval.

'Exceptional circumstances have been created for the organisation and delivery of the Competition as originally scheduled, making the postponement necessary in the interest of all participating teams, officials, spectators and other stakeholders.'

Here’s what happened in crypto today

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, crypto exchanges started blocking accounts linked to a suspected $133 million theft in Europe, North Korea denied involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin faced questions over continued wallet transfers following a $46 million security breach.

Kraken has restricted accounts linked to a suspected crypto theft of more than $133 million from an unidentified European Union entity and is working with law enforcement to support recovery efforts.

“This incident did not originate on the Kraken platform, and we’re actively investigating reports that some of the assets were subsequently moved through accounts on our platform,” Kraken told Cointelegraph. “We’ve restricted the accounts in question and are working with law enforcement to support recovery efforts.”

Kraken’s statement follows blockchain investigator ZachXBT’s disclosure on Tuesday that he identified another $27 million in withdrawals from centralized exchanges, increasing the suspected losses from the $106 million he reported on Monday. He said some withdrawals occurred after his initial disclosure.

Bitquery reported that the suspected stolen funds were withdrawn from accounts at Kraken, Crypto.com and OKX. It also found that $62.98 million in stablecoins was converted into 27,553 Ether (ETH) within roughly four hours.

Crypto.com told Cointelegraph that it had taken steps to prevent funds associated with the incident from moving through its platform.

OKX acknowledged Cointelegraph’s request for comment and said it was looking into the matter.

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied North Korea’s involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and appeared to suggest, without evidence, that the US could have been behind the theft.

In a statement released via the Korean Central News Agency (KCNA) on Tuesday, Kim rejected the FBI’s attribution of the attack to a North Korean-backed hacking group.

“The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a ‘cyber criminal state’, claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization ‘supported’ by the DPRK was involved in the incident,” said Kim. “But the US assertion is an absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

She described the allegations as a political provocation intended to damage North Korea’s international standing. The FBI also blamed North Korea for the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, an accusation Kim also rejected on Tuesday.

“This is not the first time that the U.S. has attempted to spread misunderstanding of the DPRK in the international community, blindly linking the DPRK with the ‘cyber crime’ incidents whenever they occur on an international scale,” said Kim.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest cryptocurrency exchanges, confirmed a security incident on Monday that blockchain analysts estimated drained more than $46 million from its wallets. Despite the company’s announcement that it had suspended deposits and withdrawals, addresses associated with the incident continued to receive funds for hours afterward.

Senior security engineer at Certik, Dickson Wu, published an initial post-mortem of the incident, saying the attackers “picked at eight small chains for four hours,” including Base, Optimism and Arbitrum. The attack then moved to the company’s main hot wallets before transferring tiny amounts of Ether (ETH) from 27,160 different addresses.

“So whoever did this can also move money out of MB’s customer deposit addresses,” he wrote.

Security firms have stated the hack was carried out through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit.

A spokesperson for Mercado Bitcoin told Cointelegraph that customers had suffered no losses and no data had been leaked, but the company did not respond to requests for comment on the continued transfers. A data breach could also trigger notification requirements to Brazil’s data protection authority.

The hack has not yet been attributed to an organized group and remains under investigation.

Iboss Unveils AI DLP for Real-Time Data Controls Across Cloud and AI

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

iboss announced AI DLP on November 3, 2026, a data-loss prevention product designed to inspect content in real time across AI tools, cloud applications and other online destinations. The company plans to open a private preview in December 2026.

The product targets limitations in conventional data-loss prevention. Rules based on regular expressions and pattern matching look for formats defined in advance, such as an account number or ID string, while label-dependent controls can miss files that were labeled incorrectly or never labeled. Examples include a file shared to a cloud drive, a form submitted to a web application and source code pasted into an AI tool, all of which can carry sensitive material without fitting a fixed pattern.

AI DLP combines AI and machine-learning classification with deep content inspection to examine data as it moves. File analysis and optical character recognition extend that inspection to documents and text inside images, covering content that is labeled, mislabeled or unlabeled. The design bases enforcement on the substance of a file or prompt, including when format rules and metadata provide no reliable signal.

Policies can be scoped by user, group or department to identify, flag or stop transfers involving source code, customer data, personally identifiable information and intellectual property. The iboss DLP documentation also describes source and destination scoping, named exceptions, and responses including blocking, file capture, logging, alerts and severity assignment. Violations can roll into risk-scored incidents with event timelines and captured-file evidence, giving analysts a case to investigate and close instead of only individual log entries.

Content-aware controls require visibility into more than a destination. iboss’s explanation of AI DLP says that web-based AI tools generally require encrypted-session inspection for this level of control; without it, a system may see where data is going but not the prompt or file being sent. That visibility allows content policies to apply to AI chats in the same way as other web uploads.

The controls also extend beyond a person pasting text into a chat window. They can govern which AI services, tools and APIs an autonomous agent may use and retain a record of agent activity. This connects agent governance to the same enforcement model used for interactive AI sessions, covering data movement initiated by software as well as by employees.

Where Microsoft Information Protection sensitivity labels exist, AI DLP reads and enforces them inline. The product documentation describes per-label, per-destination allow or block decisions using Microsoft Purview label synchronization, while content inspection continues to cover unlabeled documents. Labels therefore become one policy input alongside content analysis, reducing dependence on every document carrying the correct metadata.

Reporting includes data-loss risk scoring and one-click reports for GDPR, HIPAA, PCI-DSS and SOX, intended to give auditors evidence of what was found and stopped. iboss says each customer’s traffic is processed in dedicated containers with isolated SSL keys and dedicated IP addresses, and that customer data is not co-mingled. AI DLP sits alongside the company’s Secure Web Gateway, CASB, GenAI Protection and Zero Trust Network Access services, with a shared console, policy engine and data lake.

A 30-minute online demonstration is scheduled for November 5, 2026, at 12:00 p.m. ET, ahead of the December preview. The practical distinction is between announced design and availability: iboss has laid out a single content-aware control plane for human and agent-driven data movement, and the private preview is the scheduled step toward putting that design into practice.

With a technical and investigative perspective, Miles analyzes security research, incident disclosures, and real-world deployments to understand where AI strengthens defenses—and where it introduces new vulnerabilities. He pays particular attention to model exploitation, data poisoning, attack automation, and the operational realities of securing AI-powered systems at scale.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/humana-athenahealth-collaborate-expanded-electronic-prior-authorizations-nationwide" hreflang="en">Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide</a>

Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Humana and athenahealth are expanding electronic prior authorizations nationwide in a newly announced collaboration.

Through athenaPayer, Humana is able to connect with over 180,000 providers across the athenaOne network, which the partners said will create a more connected experience between the health plan and clinicians.

“Every phone call, fax and portal log-in adds work for a practice trying to get a patient the care they need,” said Paul Brient, athenahealth chief product and operations officer, in a statement. “Prior authorization shouldn’t require care teams to navigate a different process for every health plan. By connecting health plans and practices through one solution with a common workflow, we’re creating a more scalable way to exchange information, reducing work for both providers and payers while, most importantly, helping patients access care faster.”

Athenahealth internal data show more than 6,000 providers processed nearly 35,000 electronic prior authorizations with Humana in September, with over 70% completed without requiring additional information from staff.

The collaboration reflects Humana’s broader strategy to improve care access through requiring fewer prior authorizations and making the process simpler when needed, executives said.

“Our goal is straightforward: help people get the right care at the right time while reducing unnecessary burden on the physicians and care teams who serve them,” said Traci Granston, M.D., Humana vice president of clinical innovation and trend, in a statement. “… This collaboration with athenahealth is another step forward, ensuring that providers spend less time navigating administrative processes and more time focused on patient care.”

In June 2025, insurers nationwide—including Humana—agreed to take steps to reform prior authorization, including supporting electronic prior authorizations.

Why Offerpad is getting more selective on home buys

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Offerpad said faster rising mortgage rates and affordability pressures slowed home sales in Q3 2026. The iBuyer tightened acquisition criteria, leaned more on brokerage and its marketplace and guided to positive adjusted EBITDA run rate by Q2 2027.  AI Summary

Heading into the second half of 2026, Offerpad was laser-focused on “turning its buying engine back on,” however, housing market headwinds caused by rapidly rising mortgage rates and continued affordability challenges, have led the iBuyer to increase the intentionality behind its strategy.

In a call with investors and analysts to discuss the company’s third quarter 2026 earnings, Offerpad executives said that with homes taking longer to sell, the firm adjusted its acquisition criteria to require higher expected returns on a property. In addition, they said the company has become more selective about new purchases and that it adjusted its underwriting to build wider expected margins into each purchase.

Instead of providing home sellers with a direct cash offer, executives said Offerpad was looking to serve more sellers through its Cash Offer Marketplace and Brokerage Services offerings.

During the quarter, Offerpad recorded 324 total transactions, up from 295 transactions a quarter prior, but down 32% annually. Executives attributed the lower-than-anticipated number of transactions for the quarter to the overall slower housing market.

“We’ve operated with mortgage rates above 7% before,” Brian Bair, the CEO of Offerpad, said during the earnings call Monday evening. “What was different this time was the speed of the move late in our quarter and the attention it received. Housing affordability has become a national story. With headlines describing it as a buyer’s market. Buyers weighed higher monthly payments against the possibility of a better deal. While sellers faced uncertainty about how long their homes would take to sell and what price they could achieve. We believe that combination contributed to a more cautious market as the quarter came to a close.”

In total, Offerpad’s Brokerage Services and Cash Offer Marketplace represented approximately 35% of Q3 closed transactions, with the company noting that brokerage listing signings more than doubled from Q2 2026.

In total, Offerpad acquired 605 homes during the quarter, up 198% annually. According to executives, these purchases were concentrated in markets where homes were still selling at a pace that met the company’s requirements. On the sales side, Offerpad sold 210 homes during the quarter, down 43% annually, but up slightly from Q2 2026.

“Last quarter, we said we were increasing acquisitions and we did by acquiring 605 homes in Q3. We also had additional homes under contract that could have increased that total. As market conditions shifted, we reevaluated them against our wider margin requirements and chose not to proceed with homes that no longer met our criteria,” Bair, a HousingWire Vanguard award winner, said. “Where possible, we offered those sellers an alternative through cash offer or broker services. Buying well is only half the process.”

At the end of the quarter aged inventory represented just 2.6% of all homes on the balance sheet, well below Offerpad’s 10% aged inventory threshold. The company said that this inventory is expected to provide a pipeline of dispositions through the rest of 2026 and into early 2027. So far in Q4, leaders said 200 homes have sold or are under contract for sale.

Bair noted on the call that Offerpad’s current portfolio of homes is “healthy” and that most were bought within the last few months.

Looking ahead, Offerpad executives said that returning to profitability is the company’s primary focus. During the third quarter of 2026, Offerpad recorded $78.1 million in revenue, down 41% annually, while the company’s net loss of $11.5 million for the quarter was a 1% improvement compared to a year prior. As the iBuyer looks to get back into the black, executives said they are focused on reducing operating expenses and growing Cash Offer Marketplace and brokerage services.

While the company has cut operating expenses by more than $140 million since 2022, during Q3, executives said they identified an additional $8 million to $10 million in annualized operating expense reductions, which they expect to be implemented in 2027.

“Profitability remains our No. 1 priority. And nothing about that has changed. What has changed is the timeline. The shift in the market has pushed our path out, and we now expect to exit the second quarter of 2027 at a positive adjusted EBITDA run rate,” Bair said. “Together, these actions give us the opportunity to reach positive adjusted EBITDA with fewer transactions than before.”

Offerpad executives have noted that they believe positive adjusted EBITDA will come with roughly 1,000 transactions per quarter. In Q4 2026, the company said it anticipated 400-475 transactions, but executives are optimistic the firm will reach that 1,000 transaction threshold. On the call, executives noted that the firm is using lenders to fund a larger share of each home’s cost, meaning that less of Offerpad’s capital is tied up in the homes the firm buys.

“That makes our capital go further and it helps it return to the business faster as homes sell. At quarter end, we had approximately $251 million of undrawn capacity under our inventory financing facilities, the majority of which is uncommitted,” Andrew Piper, who was appointed interim chief financial officer, replacing Peter Knag, said. “We believe our cash, the capital we expect to return as homes sell, and our inventory financing are sufficient as we execute our 2027 plan. We also remain open to opportunities to strengthen our balance sheet, including strategic capital partnerships, and we will be disciplined about the structure and cost of any capital we add.”

Alex Smola, Co-Founder of Boson AI – Interview Series

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alex Smola is co-founder of Boson AI, where he works on conversational voice AI and multimodal systems. His career spans academic research at Carnegie Mellon University and large-scale AI initiatives at AWS. He brings that combination of research and product experience to building assistants that can understand conversational cues, handle interruptions, and carry out useful work.

Boson AI develops foundation models and tools for voice, audio, and multimodal interaction. Its Higgs Realtime model supports live speech-to-speech conversations, while its broader platform includes expressive speech generation, avatars, and enterprise voice agents. In this interview, Smola discusses conversational intelligence, multilingual performance, inference economics, and the safeguards needed to move voice agents into real business workflows.

Your career has taken you from academic research at Carnegie Mellon University to leading large-scale AI initiatives at AWS, and now to co-founding Boson AI. What inspired you to focus on conversational voice AI, and what fundamental limitations in human-AI interaction are you hoping to overcome?

Humans communicate through much more than words. You can hear uncertainty in someone’s voice or see that they haven’t understood an explanation. A small acknowledgement can tell you to keep talking. We pick up on these cues without thinking, but teaching computers to recognize them is still a work in progress.

That is what makes this area so interesting to me. We now have systems with considerable intelligence, but communicating with them can still feel awkward. I want people to be able to explain what they need in their own words, without having to figure out how to talk to the software.

At AWS, I learned to pay close attention to what customers were trying to do and whether the product helped them do it. We’ve brought that approach to Boson, with the freedom to move quickly as the research advances.

Voice and video give us more to work with. If someone looks confused or hesitates, the assistant has an opportunity to respond before the conversation goes off course. Getting those everyday interactions right is a big part of our ambition.

Boson AI recently introduced Higgs Realtime, designed to enable natural, real-time speech-to-speech interactions. What makes this approach fundamentally different from traditional systems that combine speech recognition, language models, and text-to-speech, and why does that distinction matter for the future of AI assistants?

In a traditional pipeline, speech recognition turns your words into text, a language model generates an answer, and a speech model reads it aloud. Each step depends on the one before it, which can make the conversation feel slow and disjointed.

Higgs Realtime is an audio-native speech-to-speech model, built for live interaction. Its training addresses conversational behavior, including turn-taking and responding to changing requests. That’s very different from putting a voice on a text chatbot.

Think about when you ask someone a difficult question. They might say, “Let me look that up,” while they work on it. You know they’ve heard you and are working on it. An AI assistant needs to do the same while it retrieves information or uses a tool, so you’re not left wondering whether it understood you or stopped working.

That becomes even more important with longer tasks. The assistant needs to keep you informed as it works and respond if you ask a question or change your mind.

One of the biggest challenges in conversational AI is knowing when to speak, when to listen, and how to handle interruptions or changes in direction. How is Boson AI addressing these challenges, and what technical breakthroughs are still needed before AI conversations feel genuinely natural?

An acknowledgment and an interruption can sound quite similar but they need different responses. If someone says “yeah” while you’re talking, they may want you to keep going. If they correct a digit in their phone number, you need to stop and use the right number. An AI assistant should be able to tell the difference.

Higgs Realtime uses the meaning of what someone says to help decide whether they’ve finished speaking or are taking a pause. Responding quickly is important, but so is knowing how to respond.

Getting that right consistently is still quite difficult. People don’t speak in neat turns and the same words can mean different things depending on the conversation. The assistant has to understand what changed and remember what it was doing.

Boson AI has developed benchmarks such as IHBench to measure how voice agents recover from interruptions. What have these evaluations revealed about the weaknesses of today’s leading voice models, and how should the industry rethink the way it measures conversational intelligence?

We built IHBench to test what happens after someone interrupts a voice agent. If you correct something halfway through a booking, the agent should take the correction and carry on from the right point. You should not have to start again, or sit through information it has already given you. The results varied a lot depending on the model and the type of interruption. One finding we did not especially enjoy publishing: the closed-weight systems generally handled interruptions better, and they held up better the deeper into a conversation the interruption landed.

That is a real gap in the open ecosystem, and we would rather measure it in public than pretend it isn’t there. It is also most of the reason the benchmark exists. You cannot close a gap that nobody has agreed how to measure. We published that benchmark with no model of our own in it. When we did put one in, Higgs Realtime scored 71.2 on recovery quality, ahead of GPT Realtime 1.5 at 65.5, GPT Realtime 2 at 62.4 and Gemini 3.1 Flash Live at 61.1. So the gap is closable, and an open-weight model closed it.

I would rather show that on a test we designed to be hard and published before we had anything to show on it. More broadly, I think the field measures the wrong things. A voice can sound completely natural and still fail at the job, because the test that matters is whether the agent finishes the task. If you have to keep reminding it what you were doing, how good the voice sounds stops mattering very quickly.

There is a significant difference between an impressive voice AI demonstration and an autonomous agent capable of handling real customer interactions. What separates the two, and which capabilities must improve before enterprises can confidently deploy voice agents in business-critical workflows?

When was the last time you enjoyed calling your cable company? For most people the answer is never. You get handed from one person to the next, and each time you explain your name, your date of birth and your problem again, so that the next person can ask you for all of it again. So take the fourth call about the same problem. You want the agent to know what happened on the first three. If restarting the router hasn’t worked, perhaps it is time to send a technician, and you should not have to make that case yourself. For the agent to do that, it needs your account history and access to the system that books the visit.

It also has to cope when the booking fails, and know when to hand you to a person who can actually fix it. Those are the situations a business needs to test before it puts an agent on customer calls. Then there is the cost of running it. A system that handles a demo beautifully can be far too expensive to run for thousands of calls a day, so efficiency is part of being ready for business rather than a separate concern.

We have been running this exact scenario with a global insurance company for several months. It is not a trial sitting alongside the real system. It handles live customer conversations, inbound and outbound, next to their human sales agents, in their contact centre. Thousands of concurrent calls, at roughly a third of the cost of a human agent.

The part I find more interesting than the cost is that it improved in place. Their own evaluation scored our conversation quality at 30 when we started. Five months later it was at 80, which is the score they use for a qualified human telemarketer. GPT-4o Realtime scored 55 on the same evaluation. We did not tune our way to that in a lab. The system got better against the traffic it was actually handling, which is the only kind of improvement a contact centre cares about.

As voice agents move beyond answering questions to executing tasks, accessing customer information, and interacting with enterprise systems, how should developers balance autonomy with human oversight? What safeguards are essential to establishing long-term trust in these systems?

I’d start by asking what you’re trusting the agent to do. Letting it look up an account balance is very different from allowing it to move money or cancel a service. The permissions should reflect that, and the systems it connects to need to enforce them. You can’t leave all of that to an instruction in a prompt.

People should also know what they’re agreeing to. Before an agent makes a change that would be tough to undo, it should explain the consequences and ask for confirmation. That way when it gets stuck, getting a person involved should be straightforward.

Things will still go wrong. When they do, you need to be able to see what the agent did and where it stopped, so someone can put it right. A voice that sounds confident can make us assume a system knows what it’s doing but we need to earn that confidence through how reliably it behaves.

Higgs Realtime supports more than 100 languages, including the ability to switch languages during a conversation. What are the biggest technical challenges in delivering consistent conversational intelligence across different languages, accents, and communication styles, particularly when understanding emotional context and user intent?

Language coverage sounds like a list, and it isn’t. A while ago a customer needed Thai. We had partial coverage, and the first thing we discovered was that we knew absolutely nothing about Thai proper, starting with the fact that written Thai essentially has no punctuation. That breaks the sentence-boundary assumptions most speech systems are built on. We could have deferred the market. Instead we found computer scientists who spoke Thai and rebuilt the handling properly.

About six weeks later we were in Bangkok demonstrating it. During the demo I could not tell how it was going — I was listening to people test a system in a language I don’t speak — and afterwards they said it was good, which was a considerable relief. Conventions differ as much as vocabulary does. In Japanese, listeners make small acknowledgments while you are speaking to show they are following along. If the assistant stops every time to hand over the turn, it destroys the rhythm of the conversation.

The words can be perfect and the conversation still fails. And how you say something matters as much as what you say. You can use exactly the same words affectionately or sarcastically, and the appropriate response is completely different. Holding on to tone and context while the language changes mid-sentence is a large part of what makes this interesting to me.

Companies such as OpenAI and Google are investing heavily in real-time multimodal AI. How can a specialized company like Boson AI compete against organizations with significantly greater computational resources, and where do you see the greatest opportunities for differentiation in model architecture, performance, and inference economics?

Having more compute does not automatically mean you are using it well. In a live conversation you can spend a great deal of money running a very powerful model just to process someone saying “hmm,” or “yeah, probably.” That model will use a lot of its intelligence listening to filler. Brains are expensive, they eat a lot of energy, and so does inference. In the end the customer pays for it.

Humans solve this by dividing attention. You can be in a coffee shop having a slightly boring conversation while the back of your mind is working out what to cook for dinner. The front of your mind carries the conversation. Then the other person asks you something genuinely difficult and you are fully immersed, spending everything on it. We build conversational systems the same way.

An inexpensive model handles the front of the conversation, and more expensive reasoning and tool calls come in when the task actually requires them. The other half of it is that we bought our compute rather than renting it. The arithmetic is not complicated: take the hourly cost of a cloud instance, multiply by the hours in a year, and you land close to what the hardware costs to buy outright. We worked out the break-even point component by component and built from there. It was harder than that makes it sound and not everything went perfectly. But it takes brute-force scaling off the table, which means every gain has to come from better data and better architecture. It also turned out to protect us: components we were quoted at under $100,000 cost roughly twice that six months later. We can move quickly because we are a small team working on one thing. A researcher can take an idea, test it, and get it into the product themselves. And we benefit from the open research community and contribute back to it, which lets a small team do rather more than its size would suggest.

Your research on ProactBench explores an important dimension of conversational intelligence: recognizing what users need even when they haven’t explicitly asked for it. How do you envision voice agents evolving from reactive assistants into proactive collaborators, and where should the boundaries lie between anticipating user needs and overstepping?

Let’s say you’re making a doctor’s appointment and early in the conversation you mention that you can’t drive. A good assistant would remember that and suggest a remote appointment or help with transport. You shouldn’t have to repeat that information.

That’s what we’re studying with ProactBench – whether an assistant can use what you’ve told it to anticipate your needs and offer help you haven’t directly asked for.

Of course, you may have already arranged a ride to your appointment. The assistant should offer the option and let you decide, rather than assume it knows your plans. I’d like these systems to save people effort but keep them in control of what happens.

Looking ahead five years, do you believe voice will become the primary interface through which people interact with AI and software? If so, how might that transformation reshape traditional applications, enterprise software, and the role of screens and graphical user interfaces?

I expect we’ll talk to computers much more, often with video as well. When you can see someone looking puzzled, you know you might need to explain something differently. Our audio-video demo shows the beginnings of that kind of interaction, with AI responding to what it sees as well as what it hears.

I still prefer a keyboard for writing code, and if I’m comparing information, I may want it on a screen where I can look through it at my own pace.

For business software, I can imagine starting a task by explaining what I need, then reviewing the result on screen and making changes directly. Over the next five years, I think moving between those ways of working will become much easier. You could talk when it’s convenient and use the screen when you need to be precise.

Thank you for the great interview, readers who wish to explore conversational voice AI and enterprise voice agents should visit Boson AI.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

TD Cowen: Democratic Congress Would Sink CLARITY Act for Two Years

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

TD Cowen’s Jaret Seiberg says a Democratic sweep of Congress would kill the CLARITY Act for two years, hand Elizabeth Warren the Banking Committee and let Democrats block Trump’s picks for the SEC and CFTC.

A Democratic sweep of Congress would end the CLARITY Act’s chances for the next two years and sour the political climate for crypto, TD Cowen’s Washington analyst Jaret Seiberg wrote to Bitcoin Magazine and clients in an election-day note.

Seiberg wrote that TD Cowen agrees with polls and prediction markets that Democrats are likely to take both chambers, with the caveat that polls taken a month out, which were more mixed, tend to beat those taken on the eve of a vote.

His conclusion for crypto is blunt: “Democrats will likely investigate Trump including his crypto entanglements. We see this as souring the political environment for crypto, which is why we do not expect the Clarity Act to pass over the next two years.”

The market structure bill passed the House 294-134 in July 2025 and failed a Senate cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15. If it does not pass before the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, it dies and must be reintroduced.

Seiberg added that crypto’s influence may shrink further in Congress if Sherrod Brown wins his Ohio Senate race, a contest the industry spent to lose.

The note treats Senate control as the bigger prize. A Democratic majority could block Trump’s nominees and use that power to force him to fill Democratic seats on the FDIC, SEC and CFTC, the three agencies at the center of crypto policy.

It could pressure the White House to drop its effort to remove Lisa Cook from the Federal Reserve board. Trump could answer with acting chiefs, Seiberg wrote, but that risks handing authority to career staff, an outcome “Team Trump” does not want.

Senate control would elevate Sen. Elizabeth Warren to chair the Banking Committee. Seiberg sees room for populist bills such as interest rate caps, limits on bank buybacks and a government credit score, and he thinks Warren and Trump could find common ground on some of them.

Rep. Maxine Waters would take House Financial Services, with a focus on housing and tighter scrutiny of the FHFA.

“That means a focus on housing though we are dubious that she will find GOP support for down payment help and public housing renovations. FHFA scrutiny will increase,” the note read.

For banks, the note says the election changes little: regulators will keep easing capital, liquidity and tiering rules whichever party wins. Divided government does raise the odds of a shutdown or a debt-ceiling standoff, since Trump is expected to reject spending conditions Democrats attach.

Bitcoin traded near $77,500 Tuesday afternoon eastern time, up from a dip to $76,300 in the morning and about $2,000 below where it opened the month. The price has absorbed a record $6.34 billion Binance hack, two days of spot ETF outflows and a Fed that is leaning toward a December hike.

Prediction markets on Kalshi give Democrats a 94% chance at the House and 74% at the Senate, so much of Seiberg’s scenario is in the price.

What is not priced is the lame-duck session. Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, a CLARITY negotiator, has said the bill could pass before January even if a Democratic Congress would not make it a priority after. That said, if it fails during that window, she doubts a Democratic-controlled Congress will make it a priority in the future.

According to various reports, crypto groups have spent more than $200 million on the cycle to avoid the outcome Seiberg now expects. The CFTC, for its part, moved without Congress on Tuesday, clearing equity perpetual futures for U.S. exchanges. If the Senate and Congress flips, that agency-by-agency approach, and who sits on those agencies, becomes the whole game.

Low-Calorie Diet and Supervised Exercise May Reverse Early Onset Type 2 Diabetes

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

The RESET for Remission trial, presented at the 2026 European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting and published in The Lancet Regional Health Americas on October 1, found that more than half of adults with early onset type 2 diabetes entered remission after six months on a low calorie diet and supervised exercise program.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is when the condition is diagnosed before age 45.

Those with early onset type 2 diabetes may be at a higher risk of complications like long-term damage to the capillaries (microvascular complications), adverse cardiovascular outcomes, and earlier death.

Typical treatment for early onset type 2 diabetes is often a combination of lifestyle changes, like diet and exercise, and medications.

However, this new study suggests that a supervised low calorie diet and exercise program may help some people with early onset type 2 diabetes enter remission.

The RESET for Remission trial included 96 adults from England and Canada who were between the ages of 18 and 45, had early onset type 2 diabetes, obesity, and were not on insulin treatment.

The participants had an average age of 38 and an average body mass index (BMI) of 35.2 kg/m2. They were randomized into a control group that received usual care alone and the intervention group, which ate a low calorie diet and participated in supervised exercise programs.

During the first 12 weeks, participants in the intervention group followed an 800–900-calorie-per-day diet and completed twice-weekly supervised aerobic and resistance exercise sessions, along with one independent exercise session per week.

For weeks 13–24, they transitioned to a weight-maintenance diet and independent exercise.

Both groups were supervised by physicians, and the intervention group was also closely supervised by licensed dietitians.

The low calorie diet consisted of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrate, and 20% fat. Participants were also encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids per day and given access to a fiber-based laxative as needed.

In the first two weeks, all meals were replaced with specially formulated nutritional products (Optifast). Then, in weeks 3–12, the participants maintained the same calorie intake but had more flexibility in their food choices.

This second phase of the diet included one conventional meal per day, with daily intake targets that included protein, fruit, and vegetables. Typically, this meant 60 to 90 grams of protein from all sources combined, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

At the end of 24 weeks, 54% of the intervention group entered remission of type 2 diabetes, compared with only 4% in the control group. Researchers defined remission as an HbA1c level below 6.5% without glucose-lowering medication for at least 12 weeks.

“This study proves what most physicians have advocated for quite some time. Clearly, diet and exercise can work to improve or even resolve diabetes,” David Cutler, MD, board certified family medicine physician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.

Cutler also added that this study provides a clear example of how difficult it can be to enter diabetes remission with diet and exercise alone. He stated that the small number of participants in the study also makes him wonder if these findings can be applied to all populations.

Another limitation of this study, as Cutler pointed out, was that the strict dietary limits imposed on study participants would be very difficult for the average adult to adhere to.

The study also followed participants for only six months, so it’s unclear how many would remain in remission over the longer term.

“It is worth trying diet and exercise first because they can work to achieve meaningful weight loss and remission of diabetes,” he said.

It may be possible to prevent or delay the onset of type 2 diabetes by:

The National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK) notes that losing 5%–7% of your body weight can help reduce your risk of type 2 diabetes.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is a growing issue worldwide, coinciding with the rise of obesity.

Research from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) projects that the number of people in the United States under age 20 with type 2 diabetes could reach 220,000 by 2060. This is an increase of 700%.

People diagnosed with type 2 diabetes at a younger age face more decades of living with the condition, along with a higher risk of developing complications earlier in life and reduced life expectancy. These complications can develop as early as midlife and include:

Speak with a healthcare professional about your risk of type 2 diabetes and changes you might be able to make in order to prevent or delay diagnosis.

A later diagnosis can help reduce the risk of some complications of the condition.
