Ai2 Releases StateBench to Measure Long-Context State Tracking

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Allen Institute for AI (Ai2) introduced StateBench on November 3, 2026, releasing a four-level benchmark, dataset and code for testing whether language models can track changing variables across long documents. Ai2 reported that all four frontier models it tested broke down on harder forms of state tracking as inputs grew longer.

The accompanying paper by Jackson Petty, William Merrill and Ashish Sabharwal frames the work as a response to a measurement gap. Existing tests have tended to emphasize the S₅ word problem, a difficult task based on following successive swaps among five objects, while simpler forms may be more representative of practical tasks and may already expose differences between architectures. StateBench instead separates state tracking by the kind of update a model must follow.

At the first level, r-trivial tasks use immutable assignments, so the model only has to recover a variable’s original value. Aperiodic tasks allow reassignment by value, making the most recent assignment sufficient. Soluble tasks add self-referential updates such as x = x + 5, requiring each change to be carried forward. General finite tasks add assignment by reference, including swaps, which create dependencies between variables.

The categories also have a formal basis in Krohn–Rhodes theory, an algebraic framework that breaks finite-state tracking problems into primitive operations. For controlled comparisons, the researchers derive the other variants from one general finite sequence, preserving the number of variables, distinct values and assignment positions while changing only the update operation. The evaluation set spans 8 to 64,000 assignments, and each sequence pairs one correct final state with four incorrect candidates for generative or multiple-choice testing. Problems are also rendered in several textual formats, including integer code and spreadsheet cells, to test whether surface presentation changes the result.

Ai2’s open-model evaluation included the 7-billion-parameter Olmo 3 transformer and Olmo Hybrid, which adds Gated DeltaNet layers, plus smaller systems trained on the same data. None had seen contexts longer than 64,000 tokens during training, and most were tested beyond that length. Ai2 reported that almost all performed well on r-trivial tasks, lost accuracy on aperiodic tasks as inputs grew and remained at chance on general finite tasks.

Presentation changed the results: Ai2 said the small hybrid and Gated DeltaNet models scored near the ceiling on r-trivial prompts in one format but near chance when equivalent problems appeared as spreadsheet edits. That gap means a strong result in one format does not necessarily demonstrate a general ability to track state.

The proprietary tests asked Gemini 3.8 Flash, Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Sol and GPT-6 Luna to generate every variable’s final value. Ai2 reported that all four performed well on r-trivial and aperiodic problems but declined sharply on soluble and general finite problems as inputs lengthened, reaching 13% accuracy or lower on the longest general finite cases.

To determine whether these failures came from the architectures or from pretraining, the researchers trained six 275-million-parameter models from scratch: two transformer variants, two hybrids and two Gated DeltaNet variants with different memory-update constraints. Training sequences contained at most 256 assignments, while testing extended to 250 times that maximum length.

Ai2 reported that Gated DeltaNet and hybrid models generalized almost perfectly on r-trivial, aperiodic and soluble tasks through the longest sequences tested. Transformers learned the easier levels but degraded beyond their training distribution and never learned general finite tasks, even in distribution. On general finite tasks, the Gated DeltaNet variant allowing positive and negative eigenvalues stayed near-perfect to roughly 10 times its maximum training length, whereas the non-negative-eigenvalue variant degraded immediately beyond the training cutoff.

The researchers interpret that split as evidence that pretraining helps explain the recurrent and hybrid models’ pretrained failures, while transformers’ continued failure on the hardest level after targeted training points to an architectural limitation.

The released dataset lists 68,000 rows totaling 1.05 GB. Its canonical configuration contains formatter-independent operation traces and final states; rendered records add prompts, completions, incorrect alternatives, complexity labels and generation metadata. Token-length fields and strata support selecting examples by prompt length.

The code repository, released under the Apache-2.0 license, generates the task families from canonical traces and can enforce semantic disjointness between training and test splits. It supports both evaluation and training-data generation while retaining the controlled relationship between difficulty levels.

The paper also presents a proof that fixed-precision Gated DeltaNet with non-negative eigenvalues can solve all aperiodic tasks and certain periodic tasks. Those include parity, the task of recognizing whether a repeated input has occurred an even or odd number of times, although the parity construction relies on rounding. The variant’s exact place in the hierarchy remains unresolved, as do its ability to solve every soluble task and the effects of numerical precision and adding attention layers.

StateBench also assumes a fixed number of variables, so it does not yet model conversations or documents in which new people and objects appear over time.

The benchmark’s central distinction is between accepting a long input and preserving the state inside it. Its controlled tasks make that difference measurable across architectures, but the fixed-variable design means even a perfect score would not establish robust tracking when new entities appear in a document or conversation.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

Police have launched a homicide investigation into the on-pitch collision that led to the death of a Spanish footballer.

Tenerife striker Jesus de Miguel, 30, died this morning just hours after receiving a flying knee to the chest from Andorra FC goalkeeper Jesus Owono, who was sent off before the match in Spain's second division was abandoned.

Owono, 25, is reportedly planning to apologise to de Miguel's family. He is said to be 'deeply upset' and has insisted there was 'no intention to cause harm'.

The Andorra goalkeeper was subjected to chants of 'murderer' by the Tenerife fans, who clashed with him as he left the pitch.

And the Homicide Group of the National Police in Santa Cruz de Tenerife are now investigating what happened during the match.

But other reports suggest Owono is expected to avoid criminal sanctions over the horrifying on-pitch collision, with his challenge likely to only result in sporting penalties.

In Spain, investigations are typically opened for any deaths not linked to natural causes.

Spanish sports lawyer Miguel Galan said today: 'The Supreme Court's doctrine states that excesses during a play are subject to sporting, not criminal, sanctions.'

Tenerife, from Spain's second tier of football, confirmed Jesus de ​Miguel's death in a statement this morning

De Miguel was admitted to intensive care after the tackle. However, Tenerife announced today that his condition had worsened overnight and he passed away this morning after entering cardiac arrest.

The striker was treated for over five minutes while his team-mates were visibly distressed, with some appearing to be in tears.

I tried Sonos’ latest app features – these 2 small upgrades fix big annoyances

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Home Entertainment > Speakers

New Sonos software and firmware updates are rolling out. Here's what you can expect.

Sonos’ promise to release its AI-upgraded voice assistant is nearing its “Fall 2026” deadline, and before fans can use the highly anticipated update, the company continues to make small improvements to its app and player features.

Also: Sonos Ace Ultra vs. Sonos Ace: Who should upgrade

This time, Sonos added System Health, a new in-app page to access a more comprehensive view of your system(s), though it’s only available on iOS for now. However, a new blog post states that iOS and Android users can expect volume presets, more interaction with the app miniplayer, and spoken voice prompts for the Ace Ultra headphones.

I poked around in the app and tried some of the latest features. Here’s what to expect.

If your system consists of multiple speakers, a mix of modern and legacy speakers, or multiple systems, you’re bound to experience a hiccup and be unsure which speaker is causing the issue.

It usually takes me some time to Google my issue and scroll through the Sonos Community forum, Reddit, or the official Sonos help documents to find suggestions and troubleshoot it. Sonos’ new System Health feature provides an overview of your systems, showing your network status and name, as well as the number of speakers and rooms in each system.

Also: I used Sonos’s new home theater feature – it’s a competition killer

According to Sonos’ blog post, System Health can detect a Double Network Address issue, which usually occurs when you introduce a new router to your home or plug a mesh system into your ISP-provided router. The result is two home networks, and your Sonos speakers will split their connection between them, flagging “System not found” errors, missing speakers, or connection dropouts.

System Health is in the Settings tab, under the Help Center menu.

Once you tap into your system, you can see all of its speakers, with unconnected speakers bearing a red “Not connected” badge, and wireless speakers showing a black “Powered off” badge when they’re off.

Then, you can see a speaker’s connection information, including status, Wi-Fi signal strength, signal/noise ratio, frequency, IP address, and more. You can also find its volume output, Trueplay setup status, line-in connection, voice control status, serial number, hardware model and version, and which Sonos platform it operates on.

System Health can also show you your account details, Sonos ID, Controller ID, and your current app build version. System Health comes after Sonos updated the System Updates tab, which now displays an overview of your app, system, and headphone update status. This page also links to the Sonos release notes.

Sonos’ blog post states that this feature is new and still open to change, especially if the Sonos community has a particularly overwhelming response to it. The blog post also disclaims that this feature is a first look, not a deep dive.

However, after a brief first use, I see it as a great way to quickly access all the details you need about your systems and speakers, especially if you’re doing some rewiring, moving a speaker to a new room, or general troubleshooting.

System Health is rolling out to iOS users first. I updated my Sonos app in the App Store and was able to access it, but Sonos’ blog post states that this update is a phased rollout.

Updates to the Sonos app and its player are a long time coming. After receiving several new ways to control your speakers’ volume (I think we’re up to three), Sonos is realizing that granular volume control is a high priority for many Sonos users, including me.

The in-app volume dial is cool, and the interactive volume scrubber is a nice touch, but there’s nothing more jarring than wanting to play some tunes on my kitchen counter speaker, only for it to blast at 80% from when my husband was watching football the night before.

Sonos’ latest big update to volume this time around is Presets, a way to start playback in a specific room at a set volume. I haven’t found this feature in my app’s update yet, but I think it’s because my hardware needs an update. Presets will be available on iOS and Android.

Fortunately, I did use another update to the Sonos miniplayer: swipe to skip. What a teeny, tiny but useful feature. It works just like the miniplayer in Apple Music and Spotify; when the app is open, you can swipe left to skip to the next song.

One major caveat: swiping right to replay a track doesn’t work — at least not yet. Swiping both ways seems like a given to me, so it feels odd that you can’t. Still, being able to swipe the miniplayer to the next track is better than always having the main player open to toggle tracks. This feature is also coming to iOS and Android.

Sonos’ miniplayer is getting so much better; you can swipe to the next track and easily control volume from it, even when you have the Ace or Ace Ultra connected (I tried). The in-app volume dial also works with Sonos headphones.

Also: Sonos Play vs. Sonos Move 2: Why I’d go with the $200 cheaper Bluetooth speaker

Speaking of the Ace Ultra, a firmware update is available. A new Vocal Guidance feature can announce the battery percentage when you power on the headphones, when the audio mode changes from ANC to Transparency, and which device your headphones are connected to via Bluetooth.

It feels like this update is the final stretch before Sonos 27voice arrives, as the release notes suggest. It appears Sonos is laying the groundwork for speakers to support the AI-powered voice assistant in Early Access. Once it’s here, I’ll test it as soon as I can.

Google Opens 85-Task Engineering Benchmark and Agent Competition

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google AI & Infrastructure announced on November 3, 2026 that it was releasing 29 public tasks, grading tools and an evaluation container for Physical-Engineering’s Last Exam (PLE), an 85-task engineering benchmark developed with Mercor, together with an open agent competition and leaderboard.

PLE asks an agent to complete professional assignments in mechanical, electrical, civil and structural engineering rather than answer textbook-style questions or make an isolated tool call. The benchmark contains 30 mechanical, 27 electrical, 18 civil and 10 structural tasks, with success measured through deliverables that must satisfy engineering codes, physical constraints and, for design work, cost or performance objectives.

The paper’s arXiv record lists an initial submission on October 21, 2026 and a revision on October 30, 2026. The new development is the public release of tasks and evaluation tooling and the opening of the competition, rather than the paper’s first publication.

Each assignment runs in a containerized project environment built on Mercor’s Archipelago. Tasks average 61 files and total 5,170 across the benchmark, spanning PDFs, spreadsheets, emails, 2D CAD drawings, 3D BIM models and superseded revisions. An agent must decide which documents and requirements govern, perform calculations and issue files such as engineering memos, calculation workbooks, equipment schedules and drawings in formats intended for licensed review.

Practicing engineers built the 44 expert-authored tasks end to end, including their project folders, prompts and weighted grading rubrics. For the 41 co-authored tasks, engineers supplied the initial briefs; an adversarial Creator–Solver pipeline constructed and tested the environments and executable graders from real licensable materials, after which experts validated the tasks and increased their difficulty. The technical paper says no task includes proprietary or client-confidential information: expert-authored environments were written from scratch, while co-authored files use public-domain, permissively licensed or newly generated material.

Grading reflects the same emphasis on completed work. Expert-authored tasks use weighted criteria tied to evidence in the submitted files. Co-authored design tasks combine constraint compliance with an economic or performance objective, and a failed hard safety or feasibility gate prevents the affected design from earning objective credit. The objective component addresses a limitation of rubric-only scoring: two wire schedules can satisfy the same code checks while differing in installation cost.

Google evaluated 14 models with seven publicly available agent harnesses, running each task three times per configuration. The solver containers had no internet access and allowed six hours of agent time per run; rubric judging and executable grading took place outside the solver’s container, keeping graders and reference deliverables away from the agent.

On the 56-task private test set, Google reports that every model scored below 50% on its native harness, led by Claude Opus 5.5 in Claude Code at 48.3%. The competition leaderboard ranks models using those hidden-task results.

The paper’s separate evaluation across all 85 tasks reports a high of 50.6% for Claude Opus 5.5 in Claude Code, averaging 27 million tokens and 63 minutes per task and costing $17.18 per task at list prices. The two leading figures therefore describe different evaluations: one covers the hidden test set with native harnesses, while the other covers the complete benchmark and the strongest model-harness configuration.

The more revealing result sits inside the co-authored tasks. Google reports that the strongest configuration satisfied 91% of individual constraints, yet only 41.3% of its deliverable items passed every hard safety and feasibility check required to earn objective credit. In practical terms, the agents often got most requirements right without producing a fully compliant design—the exact gap PLE’s hard-constraint gate is designed to expose.

Google reports an approximately 50-point spread across models, against a median three-point spread across harnesses for the same model, although harness choice still changed scores and costs. Individual task results also varied substantially, with an average three-run range of 15.4 points for a task-harness cell. That variability supports the authors’ use of repeated-run averages rather than single attempts.

The official repository divides PLE into 29 public development tasks and 56 hidden test tasks. An entrant submits its agent harness—the software that drives a selected model through each task—along with the model choice and artifacts produced on the public tasks. Organizers grade those artifacts, rerun the harness on sampled public tasks to check reproduction, then run it three times on the hidden tasks to determine the leaderboard score.

Entrants can use 13 Google-sponsored models free on the evaluation server, provide a commercial model API key or connect a self-hosted model server. The Apache-2.0-licensed repository includes starter entries for custom harnesses and adapters for existing agents, giving participants several starting points for building a complete submission.

The competition therefore evaluates more than a model’s final answer. A submitted system must navigate realistic project files, reproduce its public results and produce complete deliverables that survive hidden safety, feasibility and quality checks. The baseline results make full-package compliance the central challenge: satisfying most individual requirements is still insufficient when one hard-constraint failure can deny objective credit to an otherwise economical design.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind are finally here, two months after Apple unveiled the always-listening Apple Watch features. Here's how they work.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind, Apple’s two controversial, always-listening Audio Intelligence features, are now available on the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 with the iOS 27.3 developer beta. Both features are opt-in.

Apple unveiled Siri Recap and Live Rewind in September alongside its Series 12 and Ultra 4 smartwatches, but it waited to release the features until after the smartwatches launched. Siri Recap provides a high-level summary of meetings, while Live Rewind continuously records audio so the watch wearer can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds.

Also: Apple’s Audio Intelligence is priming us for its real AI wearable future

To try both features today, update your iPhone to the iOS 27.3 developer beta, and then update your Apple Watch.

Within the Siri app, users will find a new section called “Recaps,” where both the Siri Recaps and Live Rewinds live.

Also: Apple Watch 12 can listen in and recap your conversations now – genius or privacy nightmare?

Siri Recap isn’t listening for nitty-gritty details within a meeting or recording a transcript’s worth of text. It’s more geared toward a conversation’s key takeaways, like important dates, times, prices, and action items. A demo of the feature showed an off-the-cuff conversation between a parent and a child discussing college application strategies. Siri Recap took notes on the high school activities the student could pursue to include in their application, the topics they could write about in their essay, and the colleges they could consider applying to.

After a conversation, a user can save the meeting notes in the Siri app or export them to Notes or Journal. If they don’t save the conversation, it’s automatically deleted after seven days.

Apple designed the feature to exclude certain information from the Siri Recap. It includes a person’s name only if they introduce themselves or if their name is regularly brought up in conversation. Otherwise, it avoids identifying speakers. It also scrubs the audio of language that promotes self-harm, financial and authentication data, and personal identifiers.

You can enable or disable Recaps at any time through Control Center. You can create something akin to a Focus mode to automatically enable the feature once your Apple Watch detects you’ve reached your office or college lecture. Each morning, Siri Recap sends a notification to alert you that the feature is on. At the end of each day, you get a reminder to check your daily recaps in the Siri app.

The feature constantly assesses whether a conversation is relevant, optimizing its detection based on the conversation’s context, proximity, and length. It pauses summarization when a user is on a phone or FaceTime call, plays music, or uses earbuds. It won’t work when Bluetooth devices like AirPods are in use.

Double-press the Digital Crown to see a transcript of the past 15 seconds of conversation. The Apple Watch chimes and displays an animation to indicate it’s in use, then shows the transcript for 30 seconds.

Also: Is cellular on Apple Watch worth it? 4 ways people use it – and why I opt out

Live Rewind is built for those head-in-the-clouds moments when you miss a tidbit of information and can’t go back and ask what a speaker said. I think about how useful this could be in a college lecture or boardroom setting, where dozens of people’s conversations and questions prevent someone from asking a clarifying question about what was discussed.

The user can send the snippet to the Siri app to save it or ask about it (if they want to look up the definition of a mentioned term or get more information on the history of a mentioned topic, for example). If the user doesn’t act on it, the snippet is deleted. Any snippet you save is encrypted through iCloud. The audio buffer updates continuously, and unused audio is automatically deleted. It’s processed on the device and not sent to Apple.

To some, an always-on, always-listening smartwatch can sound like a privacy breach waiting to happen. That’s a fair concern, and Apple has taken steps to address privacy and security concerns with its Secure Exclave.

Think of the Secure Exclave as a private island on the S11 chip where the Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4 process audio. A secure audio buffer processes the raw audio, and a secure pairing mechanism privately enables the connection between a paired Apple Watch and iPhone.

Also: The Apple Watch Ultra 4 just hit its lowest price ever ahead of Black Friday

For Siri Recap, the S11 chip listens to detect whether the watch wearer is part of a current conversation. Once detected, audio enters the Secure Exclave, where it’s encrypted and sent to the Secure Exclave on the iPhone. The iPhone turns that audio into condensed text, essentially editing the conversation to remove filler words or irrelevant information, and then it’s sent to private cloud compute. Private cloud compute creates a recap, delivered to your iPhone’s Siri app.

This process ensures that no one, including Apple, can access the audio data.

Don’t want your Apple Watch to listen to you? Siri Recap and Live Rewind are off by default, according to Apple.

If your Apple Watch prompts you to enable Audio Intelligence during setup, you can choose which features to turn on.

On its support page, Apple said you can “turn on Siri Recap in the Watch app on iPhone.” Once it’s enabled, you can “turn it on or off at any time from Control Center.” To enable Live Rewind, Apple said to “open the Watch app, tap Siri, then tap Live Rewind and turn on ‘Double Click Digital Crown.’”

I haven’t tested these controls myself. I’d expect you can follow the same steps to switch Live Rewind off, but I haven’t confirmed that yet.

The concessions field problem that breaks pay-for-listing data models

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Contribution schemes that credit MLS data by listing totals overlook whether key valuation inputs are complete. A single MLS can require a numeric concessions amount whenever concessions are yes, improving downstream appraisal and model reliability.  AI Summary

Metering who uses listing data is an engineering problem with engineering answers. Deciding what a contributed listing is worth is not, and the difficulty sits in the field definitions rather than in anybody’s policy.

The proposal itself is sound, and it is gaining ground. Meter extraction, credit the organizations that supply the data, and reward the brokerages that send more of their eligible inventory into the system rather than less. Paper agreements stop working the moment data leaves the system, and an incentive attached to the record travels wherever the record goes.A contribution model based primarily on listing counts risks rewarding volume rather than the completeness and usefulness of the information. That is the obvious unit, and it is the wrong one, because two listings entered on the same morning by the same brokerage can differ by the only thing a valuation needs.

The RESO Data Dictionary permits three answers to whether a closed sale carried concessions: yes, no and call listing agent. The ‘Concessions Amount’ sits beside it as a number field, ‘Concessions Comments’ as free text. All three belong to the closing group, which RESO describes as intended to describe facts that occurred at the closing of a transaction rather than offers made before it. RESO’s own guidance says recording this data consistently is important, and notes that adopting the newer concessions fields is optional for any organization.A concession is consideration. Fannie Mae specifically says concessions adjustments should reflect their impact on the transaction price, rather than automatically deducting the full dollar amount of concessions. In some cases, the market-based adjustment may be smaller than the concession or unnecessary. Also, RESO’s concessions fields for closed transactions shouldn’t be confused with fields advertising potential seller concessions before closing.

RESO explicitly distinguishes the two uses in its April 2024 guidance. It moves the figure a buyer paid without moving the figure the deed records, and that gap is the entire reason an appraiser adjusts for it. Fannie Mae requires adjustments to reflect the market’s reaction to the difference between properties, naming paired sales as one method among statistical analysis, modeling and other commonly accepted approaches. Every one of them needs the magnitude.

It is not one field. View carries 38 category words, among them ‘Bay’, ‘City Lights’, ‘Ocean’ and ‘Panoramic’, with no amount and no rating attached. ‘Entry Level’ carries the floor as a number and nothing joins the two, while Fannie Mae’s condo section requires special attention to a unit’s location within its project and says nothing about view at all.So, a contribution model priced by record count pays for the half of the work that costs nothing. An address, bedrooms, bathrooms and a price make a record. The concession amount, the extent of the view and the condition detail make it evidence. Paying the same for both, or partial credit for the thinner one, is a reliable way to end up with more records rather than better ones.

I think requiring a number whenever the answer to the concessions question is ‘yes’ is a rules change inside a single MLS’s authority, enforceable at the point of entry, and it costs an agent the half minute it takes to read a closing statement already in front of them. Apply it to the other fields that carry magnitude rather than category and a contribution model has something worth metering.The alternative is a market that pays for volume. Records multiply, credits clear and the fields that decide what a house is worth carry whatever they happen to carry, because nothing in the scheme ever asked for them.The governance conversation is about who may read the record. What the record contains is a separate question, and it is the one that can be answered this quarter by any organization willing to make a single field mandatory.Nikola Gojkovic is the founder of CMAflow and writes on residential valuation methodology.

John Glasgow, CEO, CFO, and Founder of Campfire – Interview Series

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

John Glasgow is CEO, CFO, and founder of Campfire, bringing experience across finance, technology mergers and acquisitions, and corporate strategy. Before founding the company, he worked at Adobe, Invoice2go, and Bill.com, including helping guide Invoice2go through its $625 million acquisition. His work inside finance organizations shaped his focus on replacing spreadsheet-dependent processes with accounting systems that retain transaction-level detail and support automation.

Campfire develops an AI-native enterprise resource planning platform for finance and accounting teams, bringing together the general ledger, revenue automation, reporting, and close management. Its Ember agents work with underlying accounting data to support tasks such as reconciliation and transaction categorization, while Forecast separates posted actuals, committed amounts, and AI-generated estimates. In this interview, Glasgow discusses the architecture behind AI-native accounting, the boundaries between agent autonomy and human approval, and how finance teams can maintain traceability as automation expands.

Your career has spanned finance, technology M&A, strategy at Adobe, Invoice2go, and Bill.com before you founded Campfire. What did you repeatedly see inside finance organizations that convinced you the underlying ERP needed to be rebuilt rather than simply improved with another layer of software?

Selling Invoice2go to Bill.com for $625 million showed me the problem up close. During diligence, I handled more than 500 requests out of spreadsheets because our revenue accounting, SaaS reporting, and consolidations lived there instead of in a system. A company of that size was still running critical finance work out of spreadsheets.

At Bill, where I led strategic partnerships and worked closely with the CFO, I saw the same problem across customers. Payroll, spend management, and AP/AR had been modernized, but finance teams still had to go back into an ERP that was built for an earlier era.

The problem is the ledger itself. Legacy ERPs receive summarized entries from billing, payments, payroll, and other systems, so the transaction detail is gone by the time it reaches the ledger. Adding another layer of software can't fix missing data. I wanted a ledger that kept the detail and could do the work, which meant rebuilding the ERP from the ground up.

“AI-native” is becoming a common description across enterprise software. Architecturally, what makes an AI-native ERP fundamentally different from a traditional ERP that adds a large language model or AI copilot on top of an existing system?

The fundamental difference is that an AI-native ERP is built around the data that AI workflows need to act upon.

A copilot can only read what a legacy ERP stores. If the ERP has monthly summaries instead of transaction-level detail, AI can find a number or draft an explanation, but it can't reliably match transactions, reconcile accounts, or explain how an entry was created. Campfire keeps the full transaction detail and owns both the general ledger and subledgers, giving its Ember agents the data they need to actually do the work.

Another difference is traceability. Every agent action links back to the underlying ledger data and can be reviewed before it posts.

The third is the model itself: Campfire's Accounting Intelligence is trained on accounting data and writes code to perform financial tasks, making the results reproducible. That's what allows agents to act rather than simply suggest.

Campfire recently launched Forecast, which separates the current month into actual, committed, and AI-estimated figures rather than asking AI to predict the entire period. Why did you design it this way, and where do you think AI is genuinely useful in financial forecasting versus where deterministic accounting data should remain in control?

As a CFO, I’m asking where the month will land five or more days before it ends, and by then much of it is already known. Asking AI to predict the entire period would replace known numbers with guesses. So Forecast separates the month into three layers:

Actual: what has already posted to the general ledger.

Committed: known future dollars from contracts, bills, assets, and leases that have not posted yet.

Estimated: Ember's projection for the genuinely unknown portion, based on the company's history and seasonality.

That's where I think AI belongs in forecasting: the part nobody can know yet. Deterministic accounting data should control everything that is already known. Blending the two makes it harder to tell facts from estimates. If the two are blended, you can’t tell which numbers you can stand behind when you take the forecast to the board.

When someone asks how I got the number, I can open any figure and show the actual, committed, and estimated components, along with Ember's assumptions. Forecast is advisory; nothing it produces posts to the ledger automatically, but it gives finance a heads-up on where the month is heading in time to make business decisions before the month closes.

Forecast allows Ember to estimate the unknown portion of the month using historical trends and seasonality, while also explaining the assumptions behind those estimates. How do you handle situations where historical patterns suddenly stop being useful, such as rapid growth, a major customer loss, an acquisition, or another unusual event?

Those are the months a CFO most needs a forecast, and that’s why they’re split into layers. Only the estimated portion of Forecast depends on historical trends and seasonality. The committed layer comes from contracts, bills, assets, and leases already in Campfire’s ledger, so an acquisition, customer loss, or rapid growth doesn't change what is already known.

When the pattern does change, the finance team usually knows why before the data shows it, so they can provide the context. They can add a note about what changed, attach something like a headcount plan, or connect a source such as a CRM or HRIS through our MCP integration. Ember uses that context to re-estimate the unknown portion.

Every estimate also includes a one-sentence explanation of its assumptions, so when I present the forecast, I can show what is fact, what is committed, and what is being projected.

Campfire has developed its own Large Accounting Model, or LAM, rather than relying entirely on general-purpose models. What have you learned about the limitations of general-purpose AI for accounting, and what does a specialized model need to understand that a conventional LLM typically does not?

Accounting needs reproducible answers, not plausible ones. General-purpose models are good at generating language, but they can struggle with numerical reasoning and accounting classifications where the same inputs need to produce the same answer every time.

Rather than asking a model to reason directly over financial data, we have it write SQL or Python that performs the work. Code is reproducible, which matters for audit and allows companies to automate actions without requiring a person to approve every identical result.

Our Accounting Intelligence is trained on accounting data and understands things like chart-of-accounts structure, vendor patterns, and what a clean set of books looks like. It also learns from each customer's transaction history. Accuracy comes from the system around the model as much as from the model itself.

Finance teams have an unusually low tolerance for AI errors. As agents begin performing reconciliations, categorizing transactions, preparing entries, and interacting with the ledger, what should the boundary be between autonomous execution and human approval?

The boundary should depend on whether an action can be checked. If an entry is reproducible and traces to the underlying ledger data, a person shouldn't have to approve every instance. If it can't be explained or reproduced, a person should review it before it posts.

That's how we built Ember. Every agent action is logged, sourced to the underlying GL data, and reviewable. Teams set confidence thresholds that determine what Ember handles autonomously and what goes to a person, and they can expand that autonomy as the results earn trust.

People still own the judgment. Accountants decide how much Ember should handle, review exceptions, and explain why the numbers moved. The agents take on the repetitive matching, labeling, and reconciliation work. TwelveLabs is a good example: with three accountants covering the US and Korea, it cut its close time in half and saved over $300,000.

Campfire has also introduced an MCP store that allows Ember to interact with external systems such as payment, CRM, procurement, and HR platforms. Do you ultimately see ERP becoming less of a passive system of record and more of an orchestration layer where AI agents can work across the entire financial stack?

Yes, as long as the ledger remains the system of record. Historically, the ERP recorded work done elsewhere, so most of the answers sit outside the ledger: a renewal in the CRM, a new hire in the HRIS, a purchase order in procurement. If agents are going to do the work, they need that context too, so the ERP has to connect directly to those systems.

Finance teams have forecast this way for years, pulling pipeline from the CRM and headcount from the HRIS. With Forecast, Ember does that once a team connects those systems, and the ledger stays the source of truth for everything already known. The ledger remains the source of truth, while agents can work across the systems around it.

You have a particularly interesting view of AI economics because you are both CEO and CFO of an AI company serving other finance teams. What are you seeing in terms of how companies are budgeting for AI today, and do you expect enterprise AI pricing to move away from per-seat subscriptions toward usage, outcomes, or some other model?

We're seeing budgets shift from fixed seat-based spending toward variable AI consumption, and many finance teams aren't set up for that yet. As agents take on work that people used to do, companies may need fewer human seats while their AI usage increases. Vendors are also layering credits and overages onto seat fees, so finance teams need to model the potential usage cost before they sign.

I expect hybrid models to become more common than pure outcome-based pricing. The CFO in me wants a predictable base, and the CEO in me needs pricing that reflects actual usage.

Campfire raised a $35 million Series A followed by a $65 million Series B just weeks later. What changed in the market or in customer demand during that period that made you decide to capitalize the company so aggressively, and where do you see the biggest opportunity for that capital to accelerate the product?

We had strong demand before the Series A, but the response immediately accelerated after it closed. Companies reached out for demos, many of them new to Campfire, and it became clear that demand was ahead of what we had planned for.

That gave us an opportunity to invest more aggressively in meeting that demand, particularly as companies became willing to move off legacy platforms like NetSuite and Sage Intacct. Customers like Replit show why: it grew from $10 million to over $200 million in annual recurring revenue without adding to its four-person accounting team, and its revenue close dropped from eight hours to 30 minutes.

We raised $35 million in our Series A, led by Accel, followed by a $65 million Series B co-led by Accel and Ribbit, bringing the total to $100 million in 12 weeks. We’ve used it to keep up with that demand, growing from 10 people to more than 150 across San Francisco, New York, and London.

AI-native accounting has quickly become a competitive category, with companies such as Campfire and Rillet challenging established platforms like QuickBooks, NetSuite, and Sage Intacct. If we look five years ahead, which parts of accounting and ERP do you expect AI agents to handle almost entirely, and which responsibilities will remain firmly with human finance professionals?

I expect agents to handle most of the routine work: matching transactions to payments, labeling transactions, reconciliations, accruals, and the mechanical parts of close and reporting. It's repetitive, rules-based work that software can learn.

What stays with people is judgment and accountability. Accountants will decide how much agents can handle autonomously, review exceptions, explain why the numbers moved, and advise the business on what to do next. Someone still has to answer for the books.

Over time, "AI-native" will become a baseline expectation for financial software. The differentiator will be whether finance teams trust the system with their ledger and can see exactly how it reached its conclusions. That's the bar we built Campfire to clear, and more than 400 companies, including Cursor, Clay, Polymarket, and PostHog, already run their books on it.

Thank you for the great interview, readers who wish to learn more about AI-native accounting and enterprise resource planning should visit Campfire.

Antoine is a visionary leader and founding partner of Unite.AI, driven by an unwavering passion for shaping and promoting the future of AI and robotics. A serial entrepreneur, he believes that AI will be as disruptive to society as electricity, and is often caught raving about the potential of disruptive technologies and AGI.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

How to Test Your Home’s Water Quality

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Old Houses AREN’T Scary: What We’ve Learned & Loved About Them

Published Nov 3, 2026 12:05 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

We use water every day for drinking, cooking, cleaning, and bathing, but how often do we stop to think about what’s actually in it? Water that looks clean can contain potentially harmful contaminants, including lead, bacteria, and chemicals. The good news is, learning how to test water quality at home is pretty straightforward, whether you’re using an inexpensive DIY test kit, or you mail a sample to a certified laboratory. We’ll walk you through the testing process, explain what your results mean, and help you figure out what to do if you find a problem.

Even if your water looks clean doesn’t always mean it’s safe to drink. Contaminants and certain bacteria can be in there without changing the water’s appearance, taste, or smell. Testing your water helps identify potential health risks and gives you a clearer picture of what’s coming out of your faucets.

“A common misconception is that water is safe if it looks, smells and tastes fine,” says Dr. Eric Roy, Chief of Science at Culligan International. “But the reality is that contaminants such as lead, arsenic, and ‘forever chemicals’ (PFAS) cannot be detected by our senses.”

Water testing can also reveal less serious issues, such as high mineral content. Hard water, which contains elevated levels of calcium and magnesium, can cause scale buildup in pipes and appliances, leave spots on dishes, and make soap less effective. Testing can also help you get to the bottom of unpleasant tastes, unusual odors, or discoloration.

Knowing what’s in your water helps you decide whether you need a filtration system, a water softener, or another treatment method. And even if your water is perfectly safe to drink, you might still want to improve its taste or overall quality.

Bobvila.com may earn a commission from purchases made through these links.

Testing your water quality is a simple process that takes just a few minutes of hands-on work, depending on the type of test you choose. At-home water test kits give you quick results for common water-quality indicators, while mail-in laboratory tests offer more detailed information about potentially harmful contaminants.

Before getting started, read the instructions included with your test kit carefully. Different tests have different sampling requirements, and even small mistakes, such as touching the inside of a collection bottle or flushing the faucet when you shouldn’t, can affect the accuracy of your results.

The best testing method depends on what you’re looking for. Inexpensive, at-home test kits typically use strips or color-changing solutions to check for pH, chlorine, and hardness. They’re useful for identifying common water-quality issues, but generally lack the precision to detect harmful contaminants at very low concentrations.

For health-related concerns, such as lead, arsenic, bacteria, or PFAS, choose a mail-in kit analyzed by a certified laboratory. As Dr. Jess Goddard, chief science officer at SimpleLab Inc. explains, “If you’re making a health decision or about to spend money on treatment, you definitely want lab results behind it.”

Safe Home’s mail-in water test kit checks 200 qparameters, including 7 inorganics, 31 metals, and 20 pesticides and herbicides. Read the review.

Before collecting your water sample, wash your hands thoroughly with soap and water and clear the area around the faucet. Wipe away any visible dirt or debris, and remove the faucet aerator if your testing instructions require it. Some tests, particularly those for bacteria, may also require disinfecting the faucet opening before sampling.

Be careful not to introduce contaminants during preparation. Avoid touching the inside of collection bottles or their caps, and wear disposable gloves if recommended by your test kit. Always follow the manufacturer’s instructions, as preparation requirements can vary depending on the contaminants being tested.

For many water tests, you’ll need to run cold water through the faucet for a couple of minutes before collecting your sample. This flushes out stagnant water that’s been sitting in your plumbing, helping you collect a sample representative of your incoming water supply. However, always check your kit’s instructions first, as some tests require a different approach.

“For example, some lead-testing protocols require collecting the first water from a cold tap after several hours without use,” explains Rich Razgaitis, co-founder and CEO of FloWater. “If you flush the pipes beforehand, you may wash away the very evidence you’re looking for.”

Once the faucet is prepared, collect your sample according to your test kit’s instructions. The process differs slightly depending on your testing method:

Keep your fingers away from bottle openings and inside surfaces to prevent contamination. Seal samples immediately and follow any storage or shipping instructions.

Now it’s time to test your sample. The process will depend on whether you’re using an at-home kit or sending your water to a laboratory.

Note: At-home kits aren’t always dip strips. Some use reagents, test vials, or incubation procedures for bacteria. Always follow the instructions for the specific kit you have.

Once you’ve tested your water, the next step is figuring out what the results mean. Most at-home testing kits use color-coded charts to indicate the concentration of different substances in your water, while laboratory reports provide more precise measurements, often in parts per million (ppm), parts per billion (ppb), or milligrams per liter (mg/L).

Compare your results with the reference ranges provided by the kit or laboratory, paying particular attention to any contaminants that exceed health-based guidelines. Keep in mind that detecting a substance doesn’t automatically mean your water is unsafe.

“When interpreting results, a common mistake is assuming that a detection automatically means you have a health risk,” explains Goddard. “You need to put the result in context, first, and compare the detected concentration to a health guideline to determine if there’s a problem.”

Here’s what some common water-quality findings mean and how to address them.

Water hardness measures the concentration of dissolved minerals, primarily calcium and magnesium. According to the U.S. Geological Survey, water containing 121 to 180 ppm of hardness (measured as calcium carbonate) is considered hard, while anything above 180 ppm is very hard.

Hard water is generally safe to drink, but it can cause mineral buildup in pipes, faucets, and appliances. If your results indicate hard or very hard water and you’re experiencing these problems, consider installing a water softener. These systems reduce calcium and magnesium levels to help prevent scale buildup.

iSpring’s water softener is an electronic magnetic softener that treats mineral buildup but doesn’t remove heavy metals from water. Read the review.

Lead can enter drinking water through old pipes, solder, and plumbing fixtures, particularly in older homes. There is no known safe level of lead exposure, so any detected lead deserves attention, especially in households with children.

If a lab test detects lead, use a verified safe drinking-water source while investigating the problem. Contact your water utility or local health department for guidance, and consider having your plumbing inspected. A water filter independently certified for lead reduction can help reduce exposure, but replacing lead-containing plumbing may be necessary to address the source.

Finding E. coli in drinking water is a serious concern because it indicates fecal contamination. If your test detects E. coli, stop drinking the untreated water and contact your local health department or water utility.

For private wells, a professional may recommend inspecting and disinfecting the well, followed by additional testing to confirm the water is safe. UV treatment or other disinfection systems may also be appropriate for certain ongoing bacterial problems.

Boiling water can kill many disease-causing microorganisms, but it won’t remove chemical contaminants such as lead or nitrates. Follow the specific guidance provided by your health department rather than assuming boiling will solve every water-quality problem.

Nitrates can enter groundwater through fertilizers, septic systems, and agricultural runoff. Elevated levels are particularly dangerous for infants because they can interfere with the blood’s ability to carry oxygen.

The EPA’s drinking-water limit for nitrate is 10 mg/L measured as nitrogen (NO3-N). If your results exceed this level, switch to a verified safe water source for drinking, cooking, and preparing baby formula, and consult your local health department.

A reverse osmosis system certified for nitrate reduction may be an appropriate long-term solution. Do not boil nitrate-contaminated water, as evaporation can increase the nitrate concentration.

Arsenic and per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) can be present in drinking water without any noticeable change in taste, smell, or appearance. Both can pose health risks, depending on the substance and concentration.

If lab testing identifies levels above applicable health-based guidelines, use a verified safe drinking-water source while seeking advice from your health department or a qualified water-treatment professional.

Depending on the contaminant, an appropriately certified reverse osmosis or activated carbon filtration system may help. Check that the specific system has been tested and certified to reduce the substance identified in your results.

The Waterdrop G3P600 installs under the sink and has a digital display that monitors the presence of TDS in the water. Read the review.

Not every unusual result indicates a health risk. Elevated iron can cause reddish-brown staining, while chlorine may give treated municipal water an unpleasant taste or odor. High mineral concentrations can also affect how water tastes.

If testing confirms that the issue is aesthetic rather than a health concern, you may be able to address it with a relatively simple treatment system. Activated carbon filters can improve chlorine taste and odor, while specialized iron filters can help reduce staining caused by elevated iron levels.

“A filter that improves taste isn’t necessarily removing lead,” says Razgaitis. “A water softener addresses hardness, not every health concern. UV treatment targets microorganisms but doesn’t remove dissolved chemicals.”

Whatever your results show, choose a treatment system that is independently certified to address the specific contaminants in your water, rather than assuming any filter will do the job.

Testing your home’s water quality is a simple but important step toward making sure the water you use every day is safe. Whether you use an at-home testing kit or send a sample to a certified laboratory, following the proper collection instructions will help you get reliable results.

Remember: Not every unusual reading indicates a health risk, but potentially harmful contaminants should always be taken seriously. Once you understand what’s in your water, you can make informed decisions about whether filtration, water softening, or other treatment is necessary.

Yes, you can test your home’s water quality using an at-home testing kit. Most kits use test strips or chemical reagents to check for common water-quality indicators such as pH, chlorine, and hardness. However, DIY kits have limitations, particularly when detecting potentially harmful contaminants at very low concentrations. For more comprehensive testing, the EPA recommends using a state-certified drinking water laboratory.

Common signs of poor water quality include unusual tastes or odors, cloudy or discolored water, and staining around sinks and faucets. White, chalky residue on fixtures can also indicate hard water. However, these symptoms don’t necessarily mean your water is unsafe. According to the CDC, harmful germs and chemicals may be present without affecting how water looks, tastes, or smells.

At-home water tests are a convenient, inexpensive way to screen for basic water-quality issues, such as hardness, pH, and chlorine levels. However, laboratory testing is generally the better option if you’re concerned about contaminants such as lead, arsenic, PFAS, or bacteria. These tests require greater accuracy and sensitivity than most DIY kits can provide. For example, the CDC recommends using a state-certified laboratory to test private well water.

There’s no single list of five indicators that covers every drinking-water concern, but homeowners should pay particular attention to total coliform bacteria, nitrates, pH, total dissolved solids (TDS), and lead.

Old Houses AREN’T Scary: What We’ve Learned and Loved About Them

From crown molding and built-ins to creaky floors and drafty windows, old houses are full of charm and challenges. So we’re celebrating all the things that make old homes worth loving, living in, and fixing up. Learn about historic house styles; find practical advice for maintaining your old home; and discover quirky, forgotten features you simply won’t find in new builds.

He started writing for BobVila.com in April 2021, focusing on buyer’s guides for the full range of tools, furniture, DIY supplies, and home and garden products that the team reviews. He has worked in the translation and localization industry for the past 10 years.

The FN Mauser Is the Best Deer Rifle that History Forgot

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 12:01 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

Hunting rifles come and go and sometimes even good ones don’t stay around. The decades following World War II saw a boom in hunters and a voracious appetite for new rifles and cartridges. This was the heyday of the Pre-64 Model 70 and the Winchester Magnums, but we no longer hear much about the many Mauser sporting rifles made by FN and imported from Belgium.

I recently got my mitts on one of these rifles, which belonged to my great grandfather, chambered in .270 Win. While this gun undoubtedly rode around in the rifle rack of Grandpa Curtis’s truck when I was a youngster, I’m looking at it anew. According to my dad and uncle, Grandpa’s brother was a gunsmith and built the rifle for him. This was after he’d raised a family in a log cabin in Colorado during the Depression and developed a taste for July mule deer. He used this rifle to shoot a lot of deer and elk.

A family heirloom like this gives me a perspective on what a quality hunting rifle looked like in the 1950s, and an idea of how well it’s held up over time. It’s also fun to compare it to the rifles we have today.

It’s common to hear or read about the many sporterized military rifles that post-war hunters adopted. These would typically be either 1903 Springfields or remnant military Mauser rifles. But these FN Mausers weren’t sporterized — they were manufactured for a couple decades as sporting-specific guns. Many, including this rifle, were imported by Firearms International Corp. and marked as such on the receivers.

They were brought in and sold by various companies and in numerous formats. An ad in the November, 1951 Issue of Outdoor Life displays the JC Higgins Model 50 — which was simply an FN Mauser and sold at Sears, Roebuck. They had the chrome vanadium steel barrel and even came with a 2.5-power scope, for $135. Another ad, from the September, 1952 issue, for the Parker-Whelen Gun Catalog, lists .30/06 and .270 FN Mausers for $137.50 without a receiver-mounted rear sight. That would cost you another ten bucks. This could be a clue to the specific origin of this .270, as the front of the barrel has markings that appear to be where a front sight was once mounted, but there is no evidence of a barrel-mounted rear sight like you’ll see on Pre-64 Model 70 rifles and others like the Remington Model 721.

Ultimately, lots of these guns were imported in various forms and were sold by companies like Weatherby, Harrington & Richardson, and Colt. Complete FN rifles, according to what little I could find, had a specific pearl edge to the checkering on the grips, but it’s likely that not all of them did. Barrelled actions were also imported and built into custom hunting rifles.

This particular rifle appears to be either one that showed up as a barrelled action — possibly with a front sight. The barrel and receiver both bear the appropriate stampings for a matched pair that left the factory in Belgium. But the 22-inch barrel appears to have been cut down and re-crowned — as these were generally 24-inch barrels. It’s stamped with the year “1951” and bears matching 2837 serial numbers.

The stock has a handsome grain and good ergonomics. It lacks any checkering, but the fore-end has a nice belly and is capped with an ivory-colored plastic cap. The grip is capped with a similar plastic cap and its vertical orientation provides a wonderful balance of trigger reach and allows a crisp perpendicular squeeze. The raised comb is, in part, made from a separate block of wood. It’s pinned atop the buttstock and shaped to match, but the seams are still visible.

The floorplate isn’t hinged on this rifle. Rather, it’s detachable and retained by a spring-loaded button at the rear and retaining lip at the front. The fitting of the stock is very precise and, despite being bedded from the tang to the cap on the fore-end, very little bedding material is visible alongside the metal. Pillars around the action screws also help create an incompressible bridge between the bottom metal and action. The precise fit of metal and wood are things we’ll only see today on custom rifles and the highest-end production guns. Factory stocks — both wood and synthetic — are either CNC-milled or molded with baked-in tolerances rather than hand fit. The workmanship on my 1954 Model 70 is good, but not to the same level.

The barrel is a pretty standard sporter taper, finishing at .635 inches. I assume it has been cut down from 24 inches to 22. It’s a heavier barrel than you’ll see on classics like the M70 featherweight and contributes to the rifle’s roughly 9.5-pound overall weight.

The action and barrel share all matching and appropriate proof marks, and remain in good shape, with the bolt sliding like glass down the slick raceways. The bottom of the bolt handle is flattened and knurled for a better grip, and is generally smoother to operate than my previously mentioned M70. The safety switch is textured on both sides. It appears to be pitting because of its irregularity, but no other parts show similar signs of corrosion.

The receiver is topped with a one-piece Redfield mount and Bear Cub 2 ¾-power scope that uses a post-style reticle. In the early days of magnified optics, this gave a sight picture similar to that of a front post iron sight — to which most shooters would be accustomed. It was fitted with a Canjar trigger — a premium aftermarket upgrade, that breaks at 3 pounds, 7 ounces as set.

To test the rifle for accuracy, I fired four five-shot groups with several different types of contemporary .270 ammo. Where we would normally aggregate these five-shot groups into a single 20-shot group for each type of ammo, It was apparent that the system was limited, in part, by the optic, so I’m just sticking with the group size averages.

I believe the rifle is certainly capable of better accuracy than this but, considering the vintage scope, it proved itself perfectly capable of hitting a deer or elk cleanly out to 200 to 300 yards. Frankly, it’s not uncommon for us to test modern rifles with high-magnification scopes that average worse. A low-power optic itself doesn’t prevent a rifle from shooting accurately either, but this style of reticle requires the correct-sized target and a lot of concentration to maintain a consistent hold. The post covers about four inches of a 100-yard target. When the barrel warmed, mirage definitely affected sight picture too. I tried both a six-o’clock hold and a navy hold (dead center), and saw some vertical stringing with both methods.

Read Next: 10 Classic Hunting Rifles Every Hunter Should Own

For as much as we hear about classic rifles like the Savage 99 and Winchester Model 70, I’m surprised that Mausers like this haven’t carried a similar reputation. So many rifles, including the Winchester, were derived from the Mauser action itself. The rifles even commanded a higher price than Model 70s at the time. While precision manufacturing, materials, and coatings have gotten better, a rifle like this one could still hold its own today.

The OL editors pick the best deer hunting rifles on the market.

Ethereum exchange reserves hit 2016 low – As Bitmine adds another $11.22M ETH

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Ethereum [ETH] exchange reserves dropped to their lowest levels since 2016, raising hopes of a supply-driven recovery despite the prevailing market weakness.

According to CryptoQuant Community insights, the total ETH exchange reserves declined to roughly 14.5 million ETH on November 2. This decline reportedly marked a 58.6% reduction from the 2020 peak of 35 million ETH.

Over that period, the exchanges have in fact lost around 20.5 million ETH from their cumulative balances.

Notably, Binance registered a similar drop, with reserves falling 37.1% from their 2025 peak of 4.9 million ETH, with its holdings reaching 3.08 million ETH, revisiting levels last seen in late 2020.

It is also worth noting that the reserve drop also coincided with reports of Binance’s October 29 hack, which allegedly caused significant losses.

For context, these security concerns could have encouraged investors to transfer their holdings into self-custody wallets, rather than necessarily indicating stronger accumulation or renewed buying demand.

Institutional accumulation has also strengthened despite the token’s recent struggles, with Tom Lee’s Bitmine expanding its ETH holdings.

Lookonchain recently reported on X that Bitmine purchased another 5,000 ETH valued roughly $11.22 million, bringing the total acquisition to 6.065 million ETH, worth nearly $13.7 billion.

The institution now controls around 4.96% of Ethereum’s total supply, strengthening its significant exposure to the asset.

Meanwhile, CoinGlass analytics highlighted that Ethereum’s Spot netflows remained negative, registering approximately $7.91 million in net outflows at press time.

Bitmine’s continued purchases, together with the negative Spot netflows, reinforced the declining immediate selling pressure picture.

Traders on the derivatives market also showed renewed optimism despite Ethereum’s recent market weakness. In particular, ETH’s OI-Weighted Funding Rate had recovered to 0.0101% at press time, returning to positive territory after an earlier brief negative reading.

This recovery implied that long traders were paying shorts, which reflected fresh demand for bullish leveraged positions.

Besides, Binance’s top trader Long/Short Ratio revealed a considerable preference for long positions, with the long accounts representing 70.2% of top traders, compared to 29.8% holding short positions.

These readings together suggested that market participants were increasingly optimistic for price recovery despite the prevailing downward trend.

Ethereum’s defense of the $2,243 support level validated the hopes of a price rebound despite the prevailing bearish market outlook.

Following the defense, Ethereum’s market value sat at $2,254 as of writing time, positioning itself around the middle of its descending channel.

More importantly, the RSI indicator stood at 33.23, approaching oversold conditions and raising the possibility of selling exhaustion.

The Money Flow Index (MFI) meanwhile reached 37.18, highlighting relatively weak capital inflows despite the successful defense of the support level.

Overall, Ethereum’s declining exchange reserves, Bitmine accumulation, and bullish derivatives positioning favored the possibility of a rebound.

A continued buying above $2,243 could strengthen that outlook, while reclaiming the $2,385 resistance would provide stronger confirmation of a sustained recovery.

Windows 11 can run better on PCs with 8GB now, Microsoft says – here’s why

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Windows > Windows 11

The latest updates to Windows 11 offer improvements in speed, performance, and memory management.

The more memory your Windows PC has, the faster it should perform, especially when juggling multiple apps, files, and other data. My laptop has 32GB of RAM, while my desktop has a hefty 64GB, and both are absolutely zippy. Does that mean computers with only 8GB of memory, or even less, are also-rans? Nope, not according to Microsoft.

Also: How much RAM does your PC really need?

In a new blog post published Tuesday, Microsoft touted the gains in speed, performance, and memory management in Windows 11 achieved by the latest updates. These especially impact PCs with just 8GB of RAM, which can now run multiple apps and files faster and more efficiently than before.

To reach this goal, the company said it had three notions in mind.

The first was to make better use of the existing memory on each Windows 11 PC. The second was to protect the tasks that need to feel instant even as more memory gets used. And the third was to ensure that key apps and Windows require less memory. Yes, a computer with 16GB, 32GB, or 64GB of RAM can better handle more intensive activities. But Microsoft wanted to make sure that systems with 8GB could also keep pace.

Based on its own lab tests of PCs with 8GB of RAM, the company claims that Windows is now 20% more responsive and that multitasking is 40% faster than before the latest updates. Here’s how.

As more memory gets allocated, Windows must move inactive apps and files from RAM to the hard drive. When you need to access those apps and files again, you have to wait for Windows to move them from disk storage back to RAM. Normally, PCs with less memory have to do a lot of this disk swapping.

One way Windows eases the pain of this process is through compression. Here, the operating system shrinks less-active data into a smaller format so it can remain in memory. Though Windows has used compression for many years, Microsoft has fine-tuned the technology to determine how much to compress, when to move data from RAM to your drive, and when to fetch it back, especially on PCs with 8GB of memory.

Also: 5 apps I always install on new Windows PCs – most are free

As such, Windows can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory rather than moving it to storage, Microsoft said. An inactive app, file, or browser that you leave open is less likely to have to reload from your hard drive when you return to it.

Opening the Start menu, launching File Explorer, right-clicking a folder or file, and searching for an item are all tasks that need to feel instant; otherwise, Windows itself feels sluggish. To achieve this on PCs with less RAM, Windows tries to prioritize certain actions you need to accomplish while backing off background tasks like search indexing, Microsoft said.

Here’s one example. After you update your computer and reboot, Windows has some housekeeping to do. But any setup work that occurs shouldn’t affect how long it takes to open an app or file.

Also: How to upgrade your ‘incompatible’ Windows 10 PC to Windows 11 – for free

The latest version of Windows 11 speeds up these types of tasks and interactions by up to 20% compared with the January 2026 version, according to Microsoft.

However, multitasking places the greatest strain on a PC with only 8GB of RAM. To analyze this, Microsoft said it conducted two tests on the same PC with 8GB of memory. One test measured the current version of Windows 11, while the other gauged the January 2026 flavor. Here, the testers opened 20 tabs in Microsoft Edge, kicked off a 4K video, and launched large files in Word, PowerPoint, and Excel, one after another. The current version of Windows is up to 40% faster than its predecessor, the company said.

To reduce the need for RAM among Windows and certain key apps, Microsoft said that it has improved how memory is managed and when it’s used.

As one example, memory is allocated to Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook more efficiently, resulting in a 360 MB savings in RAM. This maneuver also extends to third-party apps such as WhatsApp, which has reduced its memory requirements.

Also: 11 Windows apps I replace on every new PC (and the ones I keep)

On PCs with 8GB of memory, the Widgets app loads only when you open it and frees up about 200 MB of RAM when you close it. In Microsoft Edge, inactive tabs now go to sleep after 15 minutes, down from two hours previously, reducing overall memory allocation.

Across Windows, Microsoft 365, and Edge, those improvements reduced the growth in required memory by up to 900 MB, Microsoft said.

How does Microsoft test these scenarios to see whether the latest Windows updates and fixes are helping PCs with less memory?

“We test those fixes across a broader collection of over 20,000 PCs in our labs, including current 8 GB models, using automated workloads based on activities such as browsing, working in Microsoft 365 apps, joining video calls, and downloading files in the background and even using high-speed cameras to measure the time from an action, such as opening Start or launching an app, to the moment the result appears on screen,” the company explained.

Also: Why your RAM options cost 4X more now than last year

The ability to use Windows without much RAM on your PC is critical these days in light of the “RAMageddon” crisis. With AI companies scooping up massive amounts of memory and flash storage chips, remaining supply is limited, and the prices have surged. The more memory you add to a new or existing PC, the more you’re going to pay. That’s always been true, but now more than ever.

Of course, memory is only one part of the picture. Your PC’s processor and other hardware also play an important role. Here, Microsoft found that a 2026 Dell XPS 13 with 8GB of RAM showed 94% faster multi-core CPU performance than a 2021 8 GB model. But the same 2026 8GB PC performed on par with a 2021 16 GB system. This shows that memory does matter for multitasking, but that the processor also impacts performance.

Whether your Windows 11 PC has 8GB of RAM, less, or more, how can you take advantage of the latest enhancements? For that, you’ll want to make sure that you’ve updated to the newest version of Windows. That would be Windows 11 26H2, build 26300.9818 or later, according to Microsoft. But that includes the latest optional preview build released in late October. If you don’t want to install that one, you’ll be fine with build 26300.9711 for now.

Also: The best laptops: Expert tested and reviewed

To check, head to Settings, select System, and then click About. The section for Windows Info shows the current version and build. If you need to update, stay in Settings and go to Windows Update. Assuming your PC qualifies, you should find the latest Windows 11 26H2 build ready to install.

Beyond updating Windows, you also want to keep your apps current. If you use Microsoft 365, open any app in the suite, click the File menu, select Account, click Update Options, then select Update Now.

With most other apps, you have to update them manually or individually. To ease the process, consider a good software updater program. These will automatically scan most of your installed applications to identify which need updating, then download and install the updates for you.
