<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/fierce-healthcare-layoff-tracker-2026-job-cuts-eliminations-health-systems-hospitals" hreflang="en">Fierce Healthcare Layoff Tracker—Children's Mercy cuts 1.2% of workforce, outsources RCM staff; 73 laid off at City of Hope</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Fierce Healthcare Layoff Tracker—Children's Mercy cuts 1.2% of workforce, outsources RCM staff; 73 laid off at City of Hope" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Fierce Healthcare is tracking workforce changes across healthcare in 2026. Stick with this tracker for the latest updates, and reach out to the team with any layoff news. Also, take a look back at our trackers for 2025 and 2024.

Children's Mercy, a pediatric health system based in Kansas City, Missouri, confirmed to press that it is cutting 115 positions, or about 1.2% of its total workforce.

The organization, in a statement, attributed the decision to "rising costs and reimbursement that has not kept pace." Among those affected, "most are non-clinical administrative, leadership and support roles," the statement reads.

Concurrent with the cuts was word that 473 of the system's revenue cycle and scheduling employees are shifting over to revenue cycle management vendor Ensemble, where they'll continue to work with Children's Mercy.

In April, the children's hospital announced plans to build a new acute patient tower at its flagship location in response to "increasing demand for highly specialized pediatric care as general hospitals nationwide reduce pediatric capacity." The project will expand total capacity by 25% to 30%, cost more than $1 billion and target a 2031 completion.

Rural Colorado nonprofit Montrose Regional Health, which includes a single 75-bed hospital and other clinical locations, announced this week a restructuring that eliminated 77 positions.

Of these, 43 were vacant and 34 were filled by current employees. Nearly half of the total positions were direct patient care roles, and 12 were leadership.

The system, in a release, said its volumes have grown about 10% over the past year while commercial reimbursement stayed flat and expenses rose. Its decision came after restricting hiring and capital spending, cost reduction efforts and other measures to reduce losses and limit outstanding insurance payments for care already delivered, and will save over $8 million annually.

The system said it does not have other workforce reductions planned at this time. The changes are not expected to affect "critical hospital-based services," though some service lines will be changing as part of the restructuring.

Specialty cancer care provider City of Hope filed a Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification outlining plans to permanently lay off 73 employees, effective Nov. 24.

The organization, in a statement to Becker's, said most of those affected work in business support and will receive support services like transition benefits and severance. The cuts will not directly affect patient care, they said.

Kaiser Permanente is laying off 147 workers across multiple Northern and Southern California locations, the country's largest nonprofit health system reported in Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification filings.

The reductions are effective on Nov. 20, and per the filing affect workers reporting to corporate and administrative offices.

The system, in statements to press, affirmed that the affected positions "do not provide direct patient care." The organization said some workers are looking at other openings within the system, though severance, career support and outplacement services are also being extended to others "where appropriate."

SAC Health, a San Bernadino nonprofit chain of clinics, laid off 52 more workers shortly after a prior round of 49 cuts.

The changes were reported in a Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification filing and, in a prior internal memo and more recent statement to press, described the decision as necessary to strengthen operations. Both rounds of cuts involved nonclinical workers, with the former closely following the closure of two clinics.

The organization said that patient care and services are continuing through the latest cuts without interruption, and, in an internal memo provided to employees, that no additional reorganization is planned.

Catholic Health is eliminating 40 positions across five of its locations and realigning 34 other roles, according to a Sept. 22 press release shared with Fierce Healthcare.

The layoffs affect both union and non-union positions at Catholic Health’s Administrative and Regional Training Center, Mercy Hospital of Buffalo, Mount St. Mary’s Hospital, Sisters of Charity Hospital and St. Joseph Campus.

The health system is working with affected employees to review potential placement opportunities in current openings, the announcement said.

Moreover, 34 positions within Mercy Hospital are being realigned as “volumes surge” in its emergency department. Impacted employees will be able to transition directly into current vacancies within the hospital.

“These are difficult decisions because they affect valued members of our Catholic Health family who have contributed greatly to our organization and the communities we serve,” said Joyce Markiewicz, Catholic Health president and CEO, in a statement. “We are committed to treating every affected associate with dignity, compassion, and respect while ensuring Catholic Health remains strong and positioned to fulfill our mission well into the future.”

PeaceHealth is planning to cut 150 positions across its facilities on Nov. 20, according to a notice filed with the Washington State Employment Security Department.

Bumping rights do not exist for any impacted positions, the Sept. 17 notice said.

Fierce Healthcare has reached out to PeaceHealth for additional comment.

Elevance Health is laying off 216 positions in Louisiana as it prepares for its contract expiration with Medicaid in the state.

The organization disclosed the plans in a Sept. 15 notice (PDF), in which it said the cuts would be effective Dec. 31. Louisiana’s Medicaid contract with Healthy Blue is set to expire the same day.

“We are focused on supporting our associates through this transition with clear communication, career resources and redeployment opportunities across Elevance Health wherever possible,” the company said in a statement to Fierce Healthcare. “We are grateful for their dedication to Louisiana Medicaid members since 2017.”

New Hampshire's Dartmouth Health said it notified 124 workers that their positions had been eliminated, while also shuttering 303 open roles at the Dartmouth Hitchcock Medical Center and Dartmouth Hitchcock Clinics Southern Region.

The adjustments, it said, will see executive and administrative leadership roles consolidated and "select programs" eliminated or affected, including Tele-ICU and Tele-ED, the New England Alliance for Health and some in-house employee training programs. The system said it does not expect the changes to harm patients.

“The employees affected by these changes have made meaningful contributions to Dartmouth Health, our patients and our communities,” Joanne M. Conroy, M.D., CEO and president of Dartmouth Health, said in a statement. “Some have spent many years serving this organization. The decision to eliminate a position or program does not diminish the value of the work these colleagues have done and the contributions they have made.”

Those affected by the cuts will be "offered resources, transition support and opportunities to pursue other roles within the health system," the system said.

Dartmouth Health noted that its operating expenses, particularly around labor, supply and drug costs, have outpaced patient revenues. Ahead of the cuts, it said it has already worked to reduce some expenses by reducing travel and conference participation, reducing staffing reliance on traveler positions and strategic capital deployment to expand capacity and meet demand.

A restructuring at University of Vermont Health will see 199 positions eliminated in late October, about 55 of which will be reconfigured into new positions, leadership announced in a Sept. 15 staff email and confirmed to press.

The changes are a second round of layoffs for the academic system, when another restructuring saw 142 roles condensed into 66. Leadership attributed both to state mandates to reduce spending and other financial pressures.

Some displaced medical staff will be able to apply for other roles at the system, Leffler said, and include leadership along with others. More than half of the affected roles are at the system's main medical center.

"As we’ve discussed in our town hall meetings, our health system is facing a major budget shortfall, and we’re relying on our savings to pay the bills," system CEO and University of Vermont Medical Center President Stephen Leffler, M.D., wrote to staff. "That can’t continue."

Some displaced medical staff will be able to apply for other roles at the system, Leffler said, and include leadership along with others. More than half of the affected roles are at the system's main medical center.

Leffler also noted that the organization has cut 17% of its executive positions in the past year, axed "at-risk pay" for leaders and will be withholding raises for "most" senior leaders.

Conifer Health Solutions, the revenue cycle business of for-profit Tenet Healthcare, plans to permanently lay off 1,037 employees as a result of its breakup with CommonSpirit Health, according to a mandatory notice filing with the Texas Workforce Commission.

The layoffs are set to begin on Nov. 2, according to the WARN filing, and involve workers who report to Tenet’s corporate headquarters in Dallas. Affected workers are not represented by a union and do not have any bumping rights. Read the full story here.

Hundreds of workers at nonprofit health system Trinity Health's Livonia, Michigan headquarters will be permanently laid off, according to a WARN filing with the state.

Trinity had signaled the staffing change back in June when it announced some in-house technology and information services work would be transitioned to an unspecified technology partner organization. That prior statement and the WARN filing outline positions that do not directly face patients.

"The pace and complexity of health care technology are accelerating," Trinity said in June. "Sustaining that pace is increasingly difficult for any single organization on its own. A partner whose core business is technology support gives us access to specialized expertise and innovation at the speed and scale health care now requires while strengthening service reliability.​"

The cuts are scheduled to begin Oct. 25 and run through Nov. 29. Trinity previously suggested that some of the affected workers would have "opportunities for new roles within Trinity Health and the partner organization along with transition support and resources.​"

For-profit hospital chain HCA Healthcare confirmed an unspecified number of layoffs in an emailed statement.

The system said the cuts represented "a small percentage of positions across our corporate office and support functions." It said the changes are a response to pressures like rising costs, shifting policy and increasing numbers of uninsured patients (HCA recently lowered its 2026 earnings forecast on worse-than-anticipated quarterly results).

The company said it would be providing "a range of resources including severance and recruitment and outplacement support" to those affected, and noted that it is still hiring for other roles across its national footprint.

Academic health system Stanford Health Care has disclosed plans to trim 95 employees, or around 0.5% of its more than 18,000-person workforce.

The organization disclosed the plans in an Aug. 28 California WARN filing, in which it said the cuts would be effective Nov. 2.

In a statement given to Fierce Healthcare, Stanford described the cuts as part of a regular review of its operations and patient needs.

None of the affected positions provide direct patient care, it said, and are located in its Technology and Digital Solutions department (79 workers, according to the WARN filing) as well as other administrative areas (16 workers).

"We are providing active support for redeployment within the organization where possible and opening new roles with the opportunity for impacted employees to apply," the statement reads.

Walnut Creek, California-based John Muir Health reported plans to lay off 78 workers, according to an Aug. 27 WARN filing .

The cuts will be effective Oct. 28 and span multiple sections of the nonprofit system. According to the filing, affected roles work in finance, human resources, marketing, IT, management and clinical care.

John Muir Health, in a statement, said that the layoffs impact about 1% of its workforce and "are mainly focused on leadership positions, employees in administrative services and employees who do not provide direct patient care."

The system noted national and state policy headwinds like the One Big Beautiful Bill Act and California's 2030 seismic mandate, respectively, for its decision to proactively tighten its headcount.

Impacted employees have been notified, and any who do not find another position within the organization will receive a severance package offer, the statement reads.

Minnesota insurer UCare will layoff 102 employees as it continues to wind down its business.

Per a Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification (WARN) posting on Aug. 3, the company will no longer support some of its Medicaid plans, which were sold to Medica, as of Oct. 1. As such, the layoffs are permanent.

Impacted employees include individuals working at its headquarters as well as workers who are primarily or exclusively remote.

Effected employees will work their last day in October, with one staying on until November, according to the notice. They will continue to be paid through the notice window.

UCare announced last November that it would begin to sunset its business operations following massive losses.

MaineHealth said in a statement that it will be adjusting partis of its IT and analytics departments, leading to layoffs for 83 non-patient-facing employees.

Fifty-six of the cuts are among IT, while the remaining 27 in analytics stem from the consolidation of three separate teams into a single, 36-person unit. MaineHealth said that current employees in both departments "will participate in a selection process for roles in the redesigned organizations," but that "unfortunately not every affected care team member will have a role in the future-state IT and Analytics departments."

The system employs about 24,000 people, and said it will be "actively seeking opportunities elsewhere within" the organization "whenever possible before any employment separation occurs."

MaineHealth said the reorganization was prompted by changes in policy and reimbursement, as well as technology and patient demand.

Tower Health filed a Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification report with the state of Pennsylvania disclosing plans to lay off 160 at its Pottstown Hospital, effective Sept. 28.

The system, in a statement, said that the "difficult workforce decisions" come amid a multi-million-dollar overhaul that will expand the hospital's ED, boost behavioral health services and "update portions of the hospital while realigning certain services to better reflect current patient demand and the realities of today's healthcare environment."

The statement did not outline any potential separation benefits being provided to those affected. The cuts represent over a fifth of the hospital's total headcount.

Wellstar Health System laid off 761 workers yesterday “following a comprehensive review of our organization,” the Georgia nonprofit confirmed to Fierce Healthcare.

The cuts translate to roughly 2% of the organization’s 35,000-plus workforce and excluded any frontline care roles, Wellstar said. Rather, the layoffs “impact our corporate and shared services functions, as well as certain administrative roles within Wellstar Medical Group,” it said in the statement. Read more

Novant Health confirmed to press it would be eliminating 31 jobs across the organization as part of an effort "to modernize our revenue cycle operations."

The cuts are intended to head off redundancy at the 44,000-person system, and part of a regular evaluation of organizational structure, it told press.

"Decisions affecting team members are never made lightly," a spokesperson said. "While these changes are necessary to help ensure the long-term sustainability of our organization and our ability to serve patients, we recognize the impact on those affected and are committed to providing transition support. We are grateful for their contributions to Novant Health and our patients."

Two Dignity Health hospitals, part of CommonSpirit Health, reported layoffs affecting 139 workers in recent WARN filings.

Fifty-seven of those cuts are at Bakersfield Memorial Hospital, in central California, with the remaining 82 at Los Angeles' California Hospital Medical Center.

A representative for CommonSpirit confirmed the layoffs and in a statement said the organization was "realigning our resources and enhancing operational efficiencies" for the long term. The statement did not address any potential separation benefits or services.

"At Bakersfield Memorial Hospital and California Hospital Medical Center, we are committed to delivering exceptional care and being a strong, sustainable partner in the health of our communities," the statement read. "Maintaining this commitment is difficult given the financial landscape impacting healthcare systems across the country which have impacted our business as well."

Roseville, California-based Adventist Health confirmed 132 layoffs stemming from the system's decision to centralize quality, risk management, infection prevention and accreditation functions that were previously handled at the hospital level.

The cuts span several hospitals, according to a WARN filing. Of those affected, 109 have been offered new roles at the system.

"To ensure we have strong accountability and deep expertise in these important functions, we have worked thoughtfully to create alignment of positions and centralization of roles. This new structure will advance our mission of compassionate, whole-person care—connecting dedicated local team members with best practices from system-level experts."

Central Oregon's St. Charles Health System has overhauled its leadership structure, announcing the elimination of 22 filled and 23 open supervisor and above roles "in support services areas of the organization."

The cuts do not impact any direct patient care positions, and are hitting less than 1% of the four-hospital nonprofit's total workforce. It said the decision was made in response to the rapid growth in scale and demand its seen in recent years, and a need to adjust its structure for continuing increases.

“The changes we are making today are about setting ourselves up for a more sustainable and scalable structure for the future and I want to reassure our community that St. Charles is strong, stable and committed to meeting community needs,” Michael Hartke, incoming president and CEO, said in the announcement.

University of Missouri Health Care (MU Health Care) shared news of an "organizational redesign" that includes a 74-employee workforce reduction.

The cuts largely landed in non-clinical and administrative areas, with the system noting that "in some areas, staffing levels exceeded those of comparable organizations, while in others, operational assessments identified opportunities to add positions and better align resources with patient and operational needs."

Those affected were offered transition resources, such as benefits continuation and other internal job opportunities, with the notice highlighting more than 500 other positions that are currently open across the system.

"Healthcare is changing rapidly, and our organization must evolve with it," Ric Ransom, CEO, said in the announcement. "These decisions are difficult, but they are necessary to ensure MU Health Care remains strong, competitive and capable of serving patients and communities for generations to come."

Other changes at the system include tweaks to various employee benefit programs that would require board approval "including retirement contributions," and the closure of four retail health clinics. At the same time, it noted a plan to boost other benefits like dependent care assistance and expansions of some urgent care and imaging services.

"These actions are not simply about reducing costs," said Ransom. "They are about aligning our organization with the realities of modern health care, improving how we serve patients and ensuring we can continue investing in our people, our mission and the communities that rely on us."

Lovelace Health System has laid off "approximately 43 positions," or about 1.6% of its local workforce, a representative told Fierce Healthcare in a statement.

The cuts are primarily in administrative and support positions, and the system looked to curb the impact by placing some of its employees in other open roles, the representative said. The system is "supporting affected team members through this transition," but did not specify whether that includes severance or other benefits.

"These are difficult decisions, and we don't take them lightly," the statement reads. "Like health systems across the country, we are facing rising costs, shifting payor dynamics and a challenging policy environment. These actions reflect deliberate steps to manage costs and ensure our structure keeps pace with current realities."

Lovelace is a five-hospital system based in Albuquerque, New Mexico, and is owned and operated by publicly traded for-profit Ardent Healthcare.

BSA Health System, a three-hospital system serving Texas' Panhandle region, is cutting 29 of its staff, or 1% of its local workforce, according to a local media report.

The system is part of Ardent Healthcare, and appears to be providing press with a near-identical statement on the decision as Lovelace Health System (above) pointing to "rising costs, shifting payor dynamics and a challenging policy environment." Fierce Healthcare has reached out to BSA Health System for confirmation.

UPMC, the nonprofit system that stands as Pennsylvania's largest non-governmental employer, confirmed it is cutting about 200 employees and shuttering 300 open positions that were not in active recruitment.

A spokesperson organization, which employs about 100,000 total and operates more than 40 hospitals, said the reductions are "primarily among those who are not in clinical or member-facing roles." The system will also be giving "enhanced severance pay and benefits coverage for affected employees," they said.

UPMC recorded $33.6 billion in total operating revenue across 2025 as well as a 0.9% operating margin. The layoffs come about two years after the organization conducting a more sweeping reduction of 1,100 workers two years ago, noted one of the nurse unions active in UPMC's hospitals.

"In that previous round of cuts, we lost many experienced and valued clinicians with roles in direct patient care and nurse education," Michelle Hart, a neonatal nurse practitioner and member of Magee Nurses and Advanced Practitioners United, SEIU Healthcare PA, said in a statement. "When UPMC reduces staff, those duties often then fall on nurses when we already do not have enough time with our patients to provide the level of care we believe in. ... UPMC cannot continue to prioritize branding, construction and executive compensation over investing in frontline staff."

University of Vermont Health said this week it has eliminated 142 positions in Vermont and Northern New York, of which 76 are permanent deletions and 66 will be reposted with adjusted job descriptions.

The academic system said most of the changes weren't in areas providing direct patient care, but that some comprised "targeted clinical changes where necessary." It added that more employees were informed that their positions or departments are in the midst of reorganization.

The organization attributed its decisions to "significant financial shortfalls."

“These are extremely difficult decisions because of their impact on our valued colleagues,” UVM Health CEO Stephen Leffler, M.D., said in a release on the cuts. “We must make these hard choices to ensure we can continue to provide high-quality care that is accessible and more affordable for the communities we serve.”

UVM Health said it's working with employees' unions through the layoffs, and that those affected are being offered transition support and a chance to apply to other roles. Its cuts follow smaller cuts at its Northern New York earlier this year that affected nine workers, and a more substantial round last July that hit 146 positions.

Centra Health, a nonprofit system serving central and southern Virginia, shared news of a restructuring that will lead to "approximately 90" layoffs, or about 1% of its total workforce.

Of those affected, the system said 35 are being offered other open positions for which they are qualified. Eligible employees will receive severance as well as continued healthcare coverage under COBRA. Centra added that it has "used natural attrition" over the past several months, deciding not to refill positions as former employees left.

Centra said the restructure is part of a broader initiative focused on spotting redundancies and efficiencies, "particularly in administrative functions," and will help "adapt to the significant pressures facing healthcare providers including reductions in government reimbursements, economic challenges, shifting demographics and evolving technologies."

Baptist Health has shared word that it is eliminating around 70 workers in Arkansas' Fort Smith area.

A statement on the decision shared with press pointed to "a sustained decrease in service volumes and the duplication of certain roles." The system added that the cuts will not affect care services, and that those impacted will receive severance and job placement services.

Of note, the news comes less than two months after Baptist announced plans to ax 150 other employees in the same area as a result of service line changes.

Optum will layoff 58 workers across three divisions based in the Garden State, according to Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification (WARN) Act notices.

The largest share of those layoffs are at Optum Services, where 36 positions will be eliminated. Five jobs will be cut at Optum Care, and 17 people will be laid off at Optum Select Management. The layoffs are effective on Aug. 26, according to the document.

Optum had not yet returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Washington Regional Medical System, northwest Arkansas' largest healthcare provider, announced a "strategic organizational restructuring" it said will reduce its workforce by 86 people.

The nonprofit said it will be consolidating certain management and support functions, while also redesigning its workflows to reduce administrative burdens. The decision is a response to financial pressures including heightened labor and supply costs as well as "continued reimbursement challenges."

“We are all evaluating our business and implementing significant operational changes,” Washington Regional President and CEO Lucas Campbell, M.D., in the announcement. “By restructuring our management operations and consolidating roles, Washington Regional will reduce redundancies and optimize efficiency while still providing the high-quality care our community has come to expect.”

The system said there will be no disruptions in patient care due to the changes. It did not specify whether benefits, severance or other support is being provided to affected employees.

Care New England said it has laid off more than 30 nonclinical and leadership jobs "as part of a restructuring in response to ongoing financial pressures that continue to strain hospitals and healthcare providers throughout Rhode Island."

Employees affected by the restructuring will received unspecified "resources and assistance" from the system, according to the announcement.

Care New England said it has been working to address an estimated $20 million budget shortfall for the 2026 fiscal year. Its announcement of the cuts pointed to low Medicaid reimbursement rates, rising expenses and greater demand for uncompensated care, and included calls for state leaders to back a proposed funding package.

“Without meaningful action, Rhode Island risks continued erosion of healthcare access, longer emergency room wait times, reduced services, and additional job losses throughout the healthcare sector," President and CEO Michael Wagner, M.D., said. "We urge state leaders to prioritize investments that strengthen healthcare and protect access to care for all Rhode Islanders.”

Closures and consolidations across 10 Intermountain Health clinics in Montana and Colorado will hit 93 of the nonprofit system's employees, according to statements given to press.

In Montana, the system plans to close a family medicine clinic and "shift the model of care" at four of its other clinics. In Colorado, Intermountain plans to close two occupational health clinics and a sports medicine clinic, while also planning to fold an endocrinology clinic into a primary care clinic and shuttering some services at an obesity management clinic.

Forty-one of the cuts are among "leader and staff member roles" in Montana, with Colorado employees comprising the remainder. The changes are scheduled to take effect from early June through late July, depending on the site.

Intermountain's statement chalked the decisions up to strengthening "long-term financial sustainability in an increasingly volatile healthcare industry." It said employees whose roles are impacted may apply for other open positions, and that they will be given access to "transition support, career assistance, severance (where applicable) and benefits guidance."

Health tech company Innovaccer reduced its workforce, primarily impacting positions outside the United States, the company confirmed to Fierce Healthcare. Inc42 reported that the company laid off 340 employees, impacting staff in India and the U.S.

In a statement, Innovaccer said the workforce reduction was related to its shift to AI-enabled operations.

"As an AI-native health technology company, Innovaccer is applying its own automation principles internally. We are building an organization that is lean, fast and focused, which prioritizes speed and measurable outcomes for our customers," the company said.

It's the company's third layoff round in the past four years, Inc42 reported.

The company, founded in 2014, built software solutions that aggregate patient data across care settings and systems. Innovaccer secured a $275 million series F funding round in January 2025 to invest in artificial intelligence with the goal of becoming a “one-stop shop” for healthcare AI solutions.

The company has raised $675 million to date with a post-money valuation of approximately $3.45 billion following its series F round.

In September, it acquired Story Health, a digital specialty care platform with health system inroads. That deal was preceded by a number of other acquisitions, including Humbi AI, an actuarial software, services and analytics company, in January 2025 and two companies in 2024: Cured, a digital marketing and CRM platform for healthcare, and Pharmacy Quality Solutions, a pharmacy-payer performance technology.

Forty jobs are in the firing line of a rehashed behavioral health model at Providence Sacred Heart Medical Center.

The Spokane, Washington facility will transition emergency department psych triage services into a new department staffed by licensed therapists beginning July 14, Providence said in a release given to press. Other mental health support roles in the ED and adult geriatric psych unit will also change over to a new staffing model that relies on certified nursing assistants with specialized behavioral health training, it said.

There will be no interruption in behavioral service availability during the shift, Providence said.

The nation's largest for-profit health system confirmed Monday an undisclosed number of non-clinical layoffs.

The cuts, according to a statement, "represent a small portion of our workforce," which exceeded 300,000 people as of earlier this year. On the other hand, Nashville Business Journal, which first reported the layoffs, cited two unnamed sources who described the cuts as "significant in scale."

HCA said in its statement it is supporting affected colleagues and, where possible, helping connect them with other opportunities within and outside of HCA Healthcare. We continue to recruit and hire for a wide range of clinical and non-clinical roles across the U.S. to support the care we deliver to patients and the communities we serve."

Service line changes at Baptist Health will hit 150 employees' roles over the next 60 days.

The cuts are tied to the system's Baptist Health Fort Smith hospital, which the company said has lost a cumulative $127 million from 2018 through March 2026. To avoid closure, services including obstetrics, oncology, nephrology, pulmonary and infectious disease care have or will soon wind down, with other affiliated clinics also set to shutter.

The 150 affected employees, among which are 10 physicians, are being offered 60 days of severance, career transition support and opportunities to join other roles at the system, Baptist Health said.

"By stabilizing our foundation now, we stop the cycle of unsustainable losses and create a platform from which we can eventually grow and even add services back," the organization wrote in a statement for press.

Oregon Health & Science University said it is cutting 59 positions tied to its Knight Cancer Institute.

Most of the layoffs, 30, affect the Cancer Early Detection Advanced Research Center. The organization attributed the decisions to "a rough climate for research funding."

Nine employees at two University of Vermont Health hospitals in northern New York were reportedly eliminated in response to cost and policy pressures.

Two of the affected workers were at Alice Hyde Medical Center and seven worked at Champlain Valley Physicians Hospital.

UnityPoint Health, which employs 31,000 people, said this week it will be eliminating 207 of its IT roles amid a transition of their responsibilities to third-party vendors.

The outsourcing is focused on IT and revenue cycle functions and driven by financial pressures "like many health systems across the country," President and CEO Scott Kizer said in a statement given to press. Additional cuts are on the way for the system's revenue cycle workers, though how many will be affected has not yet been determined.

Those being laid off are being provided with severance, benefits continuation and other career transition support.

Baylor Scott & White Health Plan, the insurance arm of the nonprofit Texas system, shared plans exit the individual marketplaces as well as Medicaid, a move it said will impact 321 jobs.

Baylor Scott & White Health said it will focus on supporting the affected individuals through the transition, including career guidance, priority opportunities elsewhere within the system, and funded upskilling for in-demand clinical roles. It also noted that it continues to hire for patient care roles elsewhere in the system.

Advanced Specialty Hospitals of Toledo's May 1 closure will see 116 workers permanently laid off, effective June 8, according to a Workers Adjustment and Retraining Notification filing.

The affected workers, according to the notice, include 45 registered nurses as well as nurse aides, pharmacy technicians, speech language pathologists and other clinical and non-clinical job roles.

Greenwood Leflore Hospital, a publicly owned hospital with 25 licensed acute care beds and 24/7 emergency department, said this week it would be laying off 86 employees, of whom 40 are part-time.

The decision is accompanied by immediate and planned service line closures including an after-hours clinic, wellness center, outpatient rehab and cardiac rehab, according to a statement given to press and other online notices. The hospital is left with about 425 employees plus 58 contracted workers.

The hospital, which serves an area of Mississippi with limited healthcare access, has been teetering financially for the past few years and had recently began exploring a deal to join the University of Mississippi Medical Center. This week's statement noted that the restructuring decisions were made "as the hospital prepares for either a transition of operations to a larger health system or a potential closure."

UCI Health announced a strategic restructuring that will lay off about 1% of its workforce, or roughly 150 people.

The six-hospital academic system said its effort will be combining position and better matching roles to its developing needs. Those affected "will receive comprehensive financial support, job placement services, and priority consideration for open positions across UCI Health as part of the organization’s realignment."

The California organization cited federal funding shifts and insurance reimbursement changes for its restructuring, as well as patients' different approaches to care access.

“We approach this moment with respect and care, feeling deeply grateful for our co-workers’ vital contributions to our community and those we serve," Chad Lefteris, president and CEO of UCI Health, said. "We recognize that change impacts all of our co-workers. But this realignment is vital to ensuring our ability to continue meeting the evolving needs of the communities we serve."

Bankrupt New York system North Star Health Alliance said in a release that it will be taking "additional operational restructuring actions" despite already conducting a prior round of layoffs near the top of the year affecting more than 100 people.

The system said the "workforce adjustments" will span the organization, and target greater operational efficiency while preserving essential care access. It did not disclose how many employees will be affected.

“This is a difficult but necessary step as we move through the Chapter 11 process and continue strengthening our organization for the future,” said CEO Andrew Manzer. “Our focus remains on strengthening the organization so we can continue delivering high-quality care close to home.”

Pharmacy giant Walgreens will eliminate jobs in two states as it adapts to cost pressures as a private company under new owner Sycamore Partners.

A spokesperson for Walgreens confirmed the layoffs in a statement to Fierce Healthcare. Bloomberg reported that notices submitted in Illinois, where the company is headquartered, include 469 job cuts. A further 159 positions will be eliminated in Texas, according to the report.

"We’re focused on becoming America’s best retail pharmacy, beginning with improving the in‑store experience for our customers and patients," the spokesperson said. "To do this, we’ve made the difficult decision to simplify our organization in both the support center and with our field leadership to speed decision making and improve the service that millions of customers rely on every day."

They added that the company will support employees in the transition.

Walgreens was purchased by Sycamore Partners last year after facing declining profitability.

Baystate Health, a five-hospital nonprofit system in Massachusetts, confirmed the reduction of 117 corporate positions, which it said is less than 1% of its total workforce.

The system, in a statement given to press, outlined "ever-increasing headwinds" ranging from rising supply expenses to expired Affordable Care Act subsidies to increased demand from patients covered by government plans.

Baystate did not outline specific affected roles, but said those affected by the job cuts have been "notified with compassion and provided with support resources." The statement also described a decision to outsource clinical engineering to medical equipment management organization TRIMEDX, with Baystate's relevant team set to become employees of TRIMEDX beginning in May.

Nine-hospital nonprofit PeaceHealth filed a notice Wednesday that it plans to permanently eliminate 94 positions across its Washington facilities.

The layoffs are expected to to be effective by April 12, according to the notice, and a list of affected roles include clinical and non-clinical job titles. Some of the employees have union representation.

In a statement to press, a representative noted the cuts affect less than 1% of the system's workforce and reflectthe changing needs of our growing communities, ongoing financial pressures to reduce costs and our commitment to advancing the health and well-being of those we serve.”

The reductions come just a few months after the system cut about 400 workers (2.5% of the workforce) and closed some open positions amid organizational adjustments. Prior to that it had cut 1% and instituted a partial hiring freeze in May.

Cigna is planning to eliminate about 2,000 jobs across its global footprint, accounting for about 3% of its total workforce.

Chatter across platforms like LinkedIn and Reddit have discussed the layoffs for several days. Benefits PRO reports that voluntary retirements were also offered to eligible employees.

It's unclear how many individuals are effected by the layoffs, and if any of the jobs were not filled.

“As we drive greater efficiency across our business, we have made the difficult decision to reduce roles in our workforce," a Cigna spokesperson told Fierce Healthcare. "This decision was made with deliberate care and focus, and we are providing a package that includes a variety of transition services for impacted colleagues.”

Unite Us, the tech company facilitating referrals to social services, laid off workers on Wednesday. The move affected about 20% of staff, or over 80 people, two people affected told Fierce Healthcare. The cuts primarily hit the product and customer support teams, they said.

The company did not confirm the number of people affected, but a spokesperson told Fierce Healthcare the move was a "targeted reduction." Unite Us is providing "career transition assistance" to those affected.

"We can confirm that Unite Us remains in a strong, stable financial position. The recent changes allow us to better align our teams and investments with our core offerings and deliver outcomes for our state and healthcare partners," the spokesperson said in an email.

Horizon Blue Cross Blue Shield will eliminate 242 positions, according to a WARN notice submitted this month in the Garden State.

A spokesperson for the insurer told Fierce Healthcare that the non-profit payer is staring down the same financial challenges that are impacting the industry writ large, and that it notified employees in November that it would conduct a restructuring in early 2026 that would lead to the elimination of about 300 positions.

The individuals impacted by the layoffs were notified on Monday, the spokesperson said. The insurer has trimmed its workforce by 8% accounting for this round of layoffs and other actions taken in 2025, they said.

"We do not take workforce reductions lightly," per the spokesperson. "Employees whose positions were eliminated were treated with fairness and respect and will receive a generous separation package."

New York's Erie County Medical Center said it is laying off about 150 employees, or about 3% of its team.

The cuts are primarily in non-clinical roles, though a local nurses' union said that some frontline clinicians were also affected. The hospital is also requiring one week of unpaid furlough in 2026 for its "management confidential employees."

The center's statement placed the blame on "inadequate reimbursements from both public and private payers," adding that "insurance companies continue to deny and delay payment for patient care services at an alarming rate. It stressed that it has not fully closed any of its programs, "but has made targeted reductions in services to meet patient demands and our financial realities."

ECU Health, in eastern North Carolina, will be laying off 31 positions within its Access East program, which helps deliver non-medical and other complex care-management services to Medicaid recipients.

The cuts stem from a decision by the state legislature not to fund the Healthy Opportunities Pilot program that supported its efforts. The system, in statements to press and a regulatory notice, said the terminations are set to go into effect on March 31, but that the organization is looking at other internal openings for those affected where possible.

Hennepin Healthcare, which operates HCMC in Minneapolis and a network of primary care clinics in Hennepin County, plans to cut 100 positions and shutter some services as it faces a massive budget shortfall.

The health system is making targeted service line adjustments as part of a broader, long-term effort to stabilize its finances and protect core clinical services and care capacity, the organization said in an announcement posted to its website on Monday.

Hennepin Healthcare will be closing or integrating some stand-alone clinics and services. The health system will close chiropractic and acupuncture services and refer patients to external providers and it is shuttering its sleep clinic. The system said it will continue to do sleep screening tests through primary care.

Senior and extended care also will be impacted as older adult patients will now be seen in primary care and nursing home services will be transitioned to other systems, the health system said. Patients needing pain treatments also will be transitioned to other providers. Medical and surgical services for weight management will be reduced and integrated into the Clinic and Specialty Center instead of operating as a stand-alone clinic.

Hennepin Healthcare runs Hennepin County Medical Center (HCMC), a Level I adult and pediatric trauma center and safety net hospital.

Earlier this month, the new co-leaders at Hennepin Healthcare sent a message to workers saying job cuts and service reductions would be needed soon because of a cash-flow crisis, the Minnesota Star Tribune reported.

During a press conference Monday, Dr. Kevin Croston, the co-interim administrator of Hennepin Healthcare, told reporters that the health system is currently facing a financial crisis due to budget challenges and health insurer UCare closing, among other factors, Minnesota Public Radio reported.

The health system is trying to close a $50 million budget hole by the end of March, several media outlets reported.

Hennepin Healthcare also is confronting a projected $100 million loss in uncompensated care and a $1.7 billion reduction in Medicaid revenue over the next 10 years, MPR reported.

Pomona Valley Hospital Medical Center shared plans to cuts 265 roles—128 via planned retirements and vacant position closures and the remaining 137 through layoffs and a reduction in hours. The layoffs will take effect by March 8, according to a WARN filing.

The 427-bed nonprofit, which employs about 4,000 people, cited federal and California reimbursement cuts for its decision as well as a budget shortfall of $40 million. The plan for job reductions and other measures was put together following a “thorough evaluation of operational requirements, organizational structure and redundancies in positions,” a spokesperson told press.

The layoffs will take effect by March 8, according to a WARN filing. Those affected will receive severance and other support.

Pomona Valley said the reductions affect management as well as clinical and nonclinical roles but that patient care would not be affected. A labor union representing some of the hospital’s nurses told membership it plans to enforce protections outlined in its collective bargaining agreement, including an option for laid-off nurses to apply for and be given other open positions for which they are qualified.

Blue Cross of Idaho said it will be laying off fewer than 90 workers, a move the group said will allow it “to remain a strong, Idaho-based company” and better serve its nearly 600,000 members.

The insurer did not give details on specific roles affected to a timeline for the cuts. It had already reduced its head count by 135 early last year when the state health department passed it over for a dual-eligible contract.

L.A. Care Health Plan will lay off 225 employees, about 3% of its workforce, on or before March 13.

The country’s largest publicly operated health plan said in a statement the decision stemmed from federal and state funding cuts, and that its restructure will strengthen critical capabilities and introduce new roles to help do so. It also said it will provide transition support and resources to affected workers.

Trinity Health said it will be laying off about a tenth of its revenue cycle management team, instead outsourcing the work to a vendor in a bid to reduce costs.

The 92-hospital nonprofit system did not disclose how many workers are affected but said the reductions affect workers in 15 states. The organization also did not share whether those losing their positions would receive a compensation package.

Alameda Health System will lay off about 250 employees across multiple locations.

A regulatory filing outlining most of these lists March 9 as the terminations’ date of effect. The California organization said its cuts will span several areas, and it is also offering voluntary resignations and retirements with financial incentives.

The reductions stem from impending funding cuts.

North Star Health Alliance announced layoffs affecting around 120 of its workers.

The New York system told press its cuts will span clinical, nonclinical and management employees and be split roughly equally across its two main campuses. Those affected did not receive severance payments outside of what was obligated under their employment contracts.

The cuts come in the wake of a payment dispute between North Star and New York’s Department of Health related to the restructuring of its hospital into a critical access hospital and a co-located inpatient acute psychiatric hospital.

Vibra Healthcare will be laying off 310 employees when it closes Specialty Hospital of Portland, the state’s only long-term acute care facility, due to financial challenges.

The decision was announced in December but will take effect on or before Feb. 1, per a WARN filing. Leadership, nonclinical workers and clinical positions such as nurses and therapists who worked at the 73-bed facility are affected.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/samaritan-health-services-join-multicare-health-system" hreflang="en">Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Samaritan Health Services, MultiCare Health System close merger" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

After months of securing the necessary regulatory approvals, Samaritan Health Services officially joined up with MultiCare Health System at the top of November to create an 18-hospital, $7.5 billion nonprofit health system.

The Northwestern organizations' deal, announced last year, directs $700 million from MultiCare into care services, workforce development, facility improvements and technology investments across Samaritan facilities over the coming decade. Those investments and other "longer-term" decisions will be informed by local community needs and perspectives, with decisions headed by "a locally led regional strategic planning process," they said in this week's announcement.

“MultiCare brings expertise and resources that will help us meet the changing healthcare needs of our region and support our clinicians and employees in their work every day," Marty Cahill, Samaritan's president and CEO, who has also taken on the roles of senior vice president and chief executive of MultiCare's Oregon Coast to Cascades Region, said in the announcement.

Samaritan comprises five hospitals in western Oregon, and will also remain locally led under its new parent organization, officials have said throughout the merger process. Its addition represented a geographic market expansion for the 13-hospital MultiCare.

Samaritan Health Services and MultiCare Health System have cleared all the regulatory bars necessary to complete their merger, which will cross the finish line on Nov. 1.

The nonprofits' affiliation would create an 18-hospital system with more than $7.5 billion in revenue—and give MultiCare an acute-care foothold in Oregon. Announced in 2025, it also includes a $700 million, 10-year commitment from MultiCare to invest in Samaritan's facilities, workforce and care access points.

On Monday, the pair said that all public agencies that were reviewing their deal "have either approved the transaction or announced their intent to do so." Some of these, such as the approval from the Oregon Health Authority (see below), would bring additional conditions on areas such as the reinvestment of operating margins into local care.

“We look forward to investing in the future of healthcare across Samaritan’s service area and advancing our vision to be the Pacific Northwest’s highest-value system of health,” Bill Robertson, CEO of MultiCare, said in a statement. “Together, we can build on the strengths of both organizations and invest in the future of healthcare for the people who depend on us.”

A phased integration process will begin on Nov. 1, with the organizations reassuring patients and health plan members that the process wouldn't interrupt services or coverage. Samaritan is also slated to retain its name, local leadership and community-based governance, they reaffirmed.

The Oregon Health Authority (OHA) approved a deal on Monday that would merge the nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services.

State regulators said the OHA's Health Care Market Oversight (HCMO) program reviewed how the proposed merger would affect Samaritan's facilities and its impact on Oregon citizens' access to affordable and equitable healthcare.

HCMO approved the deal provided it meets established conditions (PDF), including requiring MultiCare to follow its financial commitment of investing $700 million towards Samaritan and posting a public-facing version of the commitment on Samaritan's website. Other conditions involve reinvestment of Samaritan's operating margins into local communities and a requirement to maintain existing services.

The proposal to form a $7.5 billion, 18-hospital system still has to face review from other state programs prior to moving forward.

OHA's Coordinated Care Organization (CCO) Form A review is analyzing the deal's potential impact on InterCommunity Health Network's financial stability and ability to serve state Medicaid recipients. Moreover, the state Department of Consumer and Business Services (DCBS) is reviewing the financial and overall impact of changing control of Samaritan commercial health plans.

Samaritan President and CEO Marty Cahill said in a statement that the approval is "an important step forward for the communities we serve."

"Our affiliation with MultiCare will provide additional resources to invest in our workforce, facilities, technology and services—and help make it easier for people to receive high-quality care close to home," Cahill said.

The nonprofits will continue to operate as independent organizations until the transaction closes, and executives say they are working toward an Oct. 1 closing.

The boards of nonprofits MultiCare Health System and Samaritan Health Services have approved an affiliation they expect to consummate in mid-2026.

The arrangement would bring Samaritan, which runs five community hospitals as well as health plans and clinics in Oregon’s Benton, Lincoln and Linn counties, under the 13-hospital MultiCare—a new geographic foothold for the Tacoma, Washington-based organization.

“MultiCare and Samaritan are closely aligned in our mission, vision and values, making us ideal partners as we strengthen our networks across our service regions,” MultiCare CEO Bill Robertson said in the announcement. “It will be our privilege to be a part of the communities Samaritan serves, and we look forward to working together to support and advance health care in Oregon.”

The systems said there are no immediate changes for Samaritans’ patients, plan members or employees as the two sides move through the affiliation process.

Once the deal is finalized, Samaritan will remain “locally governed and community-based” but receive new resources from MultiCare that will expand services, update its operations and ensure sustainable care in its Oregon communities, according to the announcement.

More specifically, the deal includes a 10-year investment commitment focused on modernizing Samaritan’s facilities, expanding the flagship Good Samaritan Regional Medical Center’s inpatient capacity, adopting new technologies including telehealth capabilities and growing primary and specialty care. MultiCare’s behavioral health will also expand into Samaritan’s service area along the mid-Willamette Valley and central coast.

These changes will together increase care quality and access while helping to lower the cost of care, the organizations said.

“This affiliation represents an important step in securing the future of nonprofit health care in our region,” Marty Cahill, president and CEO of Samaritan, said in the announcement. “Our commitment has always been to remain a community-based, mission-driven organization, and this partnership strengthens that promise for decades to come. […] We are excited and honored to become MultiCare’s first health system in Oregon and to bring the benefits of MultiCare’s expertise home to the communities we serve.”

The parties said they will sign a formal affiliation agreement “in the coming weeks.”

MultiCare Health System employs more than 28,000 people across its 13 hospitals. It has 300-plus care locations in Washington, Idaho and Oregon, though no hospitals in the latter. The system reported $5.9 billion of total operating revenue but a $116.5 million operating loss (-2% operating margin) in 2024, a nearly $70 million improvement over the prior year.

Samaritan Health Services operates more than 80 physician clinics and multiple health insurance plans, serving 110,000 people, as well as its five community hospitals. In 2024, the nonprofit reported more than $1.7 billion of revenue but a $93.6 million operating loss (-0.5% operating loss).

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/leantaas-expands-iqueue-ambient-intelligence-lookdeep-acquisition" hreflang="en">LeanTaaS expands iQueue into ambient intelligence with LookDeep acquisition</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
LeanTaaS expands iQueue into ambient intelligence with LookDeep acquisition" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Healthcare technology company LeanTaaS is expanding its iQueue platform beyond traditional hospital operations and capacity management into ambient intelligence, using computer vision to monitor patient rooms and turn signals into actions.

As part of this strategy, the company plans to launch iQueue for Virtual Care, using AI-powered patient-room monitoring to detect events such as fall risks, patient movement and other environmental signals, then route those insights into operational workflow.

The system is designed to turn events like an attempted bed exit into prioritized tasks rather than additional alarms. AI monitors rooms and surfaces events to a virtual observation team, which can assess the situation, remotely engage the patient or escalate to bedside staff when needed, Mohan Giridharadas, founder and CEO of LeanTaaS, told Fierce Healthcare in an interview.

Urgency and routing are based on the patient’s context and the health system’s established protocols. Human clinicians remain responsible for clinical decisions, Giridharadas said, while iQueue provides context, prioritizes events and helps route information to the appropriate care team.

The company’s software and services are being used by nearly 200 health systems in more than 1,200 hospitals, centers and clinics in the U.S.

To accelerate this strategy, LeanTaaS acquired LookDeep Health, its second acquisition in about two months following its purchase of care transition platform Aidin. The deal brings computer vision technology, an open platform approach and virtual care deployment experience into LeanTaaS.

LeanTaaS provided Fierce Healthcare with a first look at the acquisition news. Financial details of the deal were not disclosed.

Giridharadas said the company is building the "central nervous system of the hospital," and computer vision will be a key data source for this broader hospital operations platform.

"It is a move beyond retrospective dashboards toward continuous operational awareness and execution," Giridharadas told Fierce Healthcare.

"A great operational brain needs more signal[s], so [that] it can decide faster and decide better. Health systems generate hundreds of thousands of data points each day—but the vast majority of them are discarded after they have served their immediate purpose. The next frontier is building a robust and scalable approach to capture high-value signals from the physical care environment that may never reach the EHR, or may reach it too late to be useful. That includes whether a patient is alone, trying to stand or hasn't been out of bed in days, when a nurse was last in the room, and whether a room is ready for the next patient, from compatible cameras and in-room devices, and potentially other device-generated or workflow signals," Giridharadas said.

"The test is whether a signal is sufficiently accurate and timely, and whether it can be converted into a clear action for the care team. We intend to continuously expand the sources and uses of data from the entire end-to-end patient journey," he added.

Narinder Singh, former CEO and co-founder of LookDeep who joined LeanTaaS as senior vice president of ambient intelligence, said the technology can support stretched care teams by monitoring patients when clinicians can’t be present. "Nurses and physicians cannot be in every room at every moment, and too often technology has added to their work instead of taking it away. Ambient intelligence keeps watch when they can't be there, so staff finally feel the technology works for them, not the other way around," he said in a statement.

LeanTaaS acquired LookDeep rather than partner with a computer vision or virtual care company to integrate ambient intelligence technology deeply into the iQueue platform, executives said.

"We are building the central nervous system of healthcare operations—a highly functional nervous system must have audio and video sensory capabilities in order to be effective. This is why we needed to acquire the capability rather than rely on an arms-length partnership. We already had the intelligence that drives the car, 15 years of it. What we wanted was the best perception ever built for hospitals. Nobody confuses the cameras with the car, but there is no self-driving car without them," Giridharadas said.

LookDeep brings three capabilities to LeanTaaS' platform: peer-reviewed vision AI, an open technology platform that works with hospitals’ existing infrastructure and experience deploying virtual care. Its approach also focuses on using AI to ease care team capacity constraints rather than adding to clinicians’ workloads.

Health systems can build on compatible cameras and in-room devices already in place or deploy supported hardware as needed. This open approach helps preserve existing investments while creating a foundation for new capabilities, according to the company.

"The acquisition gives us the opportunity to make ambient intelligence a native part of iQueue and extend the technology across our broader portfolio. We also share a conviction that in something as complex as a hospital, before you can be smart, you must be open. Vendor-specific camera silos will not last as health systems demand greater interoperability," Giridharadas said, noting that a camera alone "does not generate an ROI." "The value comes when what it sees changes what happens next—earlier, with the right person acting. Nobody expects the barcode scanner to redefine the supply chain, yet no supply chain runs well without one."

The new virtual care offering includes three services: virtual observation for AI-assisted patient monitoring and escalation, virtual nursing to support admissions, rounding, patient education and discharge preparation, and virtual medicine to connect patients with remote physicians and specialists.

These offerings build on LookDeep’s experience deploying the technology in health systems. Across different deployments and operating models, reported results include a more than 70% reduction in fall rates, twice as much nursing contact, an 80% reduction in medication errors and threefold cost savings compared with traditional virtual sitting solutions, according to the companies.

Beyond virtual care, LeanTaaS plans to incorporate ambient intelligence into additional iQueue products and workflows to support capacity, patient flow and care coordination.

"We will bring the vision capability into all of our products beginning with inpatient flow and operating rooms and then moving on to infusion centers and other areas of the hospital," Giridharadas said.

The company aims to find areas in hospital patient care where important signals are either missed or received too late by current systems. "We will prioritize use cases where the signal is reliable, the response is well defined and the impact can be measured," he noted.

As adoption of AI in healthcare grows, LeanTaaS says its competitive advantage comes from a proprietary knowledge base built from more than 15 years of health system deployments. "We have accumulated an enormous knowledge base of thousands of corner cases that make operational improvement and change management difficult to accomplish. We have built a multi-layered knowledge architecture of tens of thousands of 'knowledge nodes' that enables us to deliver an enterprise-grade AI capability where the data and knowledge is tightly curated to the unique needs of each site at each customer rather than relying on generic recommendations from foundation LLM models," he said.

Keke Palmer Wore Rare Bob Mackie—and Other Vintage Gems—at the 2026 CFDA Awards

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As the host of last night’s 2026 CFDA Awards in New York City, all eyes were on Keke Palmer to deliver us a major fashion moment. The annual awards ceremony, after all, is all about celebrating American style and design. “Fashion is such a big part of how I tell stories, so getting to celebrate the people behind it was exciting,” Palmer tells Vogue today, post-CFDAs. “I love how a look can connect different eras, and bring a memory or a feeling into the present. To be in a room celebrating that creativity, while bringing my own personality and humor to the evening, felt like a beautiful fit.”

Of course, Palmer did not disappoint on that front. The star went archival for the night—as she is wont to do—bringing four vintage gems to the red carpet. “I wanted my looks to be unique, and celebrate some incredible talent from the past,” says Palmer of her fashion direction. “Everything with me is somehow a throwback or referential. That’s part of the kind of artist I am: Fashion becomes another way to tell that pop culture story.”

Working with stylist Molly Dickson, the duo wanted each of the four looks to have its own moment throughout the night. Palmer first hit the red carpet in a one-of-a-kind (and incredibly rare) Bob Mackie piece from the 1970s. The dress has a particularly noteworthy history: The strappy, shimmery-gold gown was originally custom-made for actor and singer Raquel Welch, which she wore to perform “I’m a Woman” alongside Cher on The Cher Show back in 1975. (You’ve all seen the clip.) “Wearing Bob Mackie was one of the first ideas I mentioned to Molly,” says Palmer. “His work holds so much entertainment history, and I loved being able to bring my own energy to a piece with that story. It’s classic, and it felt like the right way to step into the night.”

Turns out, the iconic piece of pop culture was sourced from the professional vintage dealer Timeless Vixen—a celebrity favorite that Sabrina Carpenter and Kylie Jenner also use for their extremely rare red carpet pulls. “When Keke’s styling team reached out looking for something special by Bob Mackie, this piece immediately came to mind,” says owner Lauren Lepire. “I’ve always adored his work, and with his recent passing, it’s especially meaningful to see Keke bring such an iconic design back into the spotlight.”

From there, Palmer then changed into her three other vintage frocks of the night. There was the black skin-revealing Marc Bouwer dress once worn by Toni Braxton; a striped, one-shouldered 1980s Halston number; And a slinky black 1970s Stephen Burrows to end with a bang. “For me, it was a story through the ages, celebrating these designers and the women who brought their clothes to life,” says Palmer. “We also told a black and gold color story, ending with a look that brought the two together. That sense of integration feels very connected to where I am artistically—bringing different references and parts of myself into one expression.”

While playing dress up was undoubtedly a highlight of the night for Palmer, her favorite moments included some unexpected run-ins with fellow stars. “You’re doing a show, but you also get to connect with people whose work you admire and enjoy being in the room together,” says Palmer. “I loved seeing Demi Moore backstage in between moments, seeing Law Roach, and catching up with Prabal Gurung. Those little moments in between everything are some of my favorites. Saw my girl Brandy, too!”

After delivering her statement fashions at the CFDAs, Palmer says she ended the night with one thing: “Bed! I’m a homebody.” Up next? She will be filming season two of The ’Burbs, and working on new music, too. (She just released her last video, “Keke Frankenstein,” this Halloween weekend.) “I’m building the world around my album, and having so much fun with ‘Keke Frankenstein,’” says Palmer. “I love when the music, visuals, fashion, and characters can all speak to one another.”

In other words: Palmer stays booked and busy. “Between motherhood, music, acting, and building Practice by Palmer, there’s always something happening,” says Palmer. “This fall, I’ve been making room for experiences that feed me as a person. The ‘play’ in me still has a way of becoming productive. #Virgo!”

DOJ sends election monitors to all 50 states amid midterm election voting

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 1,000 monitors have largely been sent to jurisdictions that voted for Kamala Harris in 2024 and are populated by Black and Latino voters; Michigan and Texas have the most jurisdictions listed.

WASHINGTON (CN) â The Justice Department announced Tuesday that it had, for the first time in American history, deployed over 1,000 election monitors to polling stations in all 50 states.

The announcement comes after a highly polarized campaign for the November midterms that is likely to shake up the power balance in Washington against President Donald Trump, as he faces historically low approval ratings over his handling of the economy, his extreme anti-immigrant agenda and his unpopular war in Iran.

Throughout the 50 states, the monitors have been sent to certain jurisdictions in each state. Of the 126 jurisdictions, former Vice President Kamala Harris defeated President Donald Trump in about two-thirds of the locations in the 2024 presidential election.

Michigan and Texas â the two states with the most highlighted Senate races in the country this midterm cycle, between Democrat Abdul El-Sayed and Republican Mike Rogers in Michigan and Democrat James Talarico and Republican Ken Paxton in Texas â have the most listed jurisdictions, with seven and eight, respectively.

New York, Illinois and Georgia each had five jurisdictions listed, while Florida, California, Arizona, Pennsylvania and Virginia had four. Black and Latino voters make up a majority in many of the 126 jurisdictions.

According to the Justice Department, the Civil Rights Division is leading the effort, with personnel from the division, other Justice Department components, U.S. attorneysâ offices and federal observers from the Office of Personnel Management.

Assistant Attorney General Harmeet K. Dhillon, head of the Civil Rights Division, announced the monitorsâ deployment as a continuation of the Justice Departmentâs long-standing practice of sending monitors under the 1965 Voting Rights Act.

âElection integrity has been one of the top priorities for this administration and this Division,â Dhillon said in a statement. âI am very proud we are deploying the largest monitoring effort in DOJ history, and pursuing transparency for elections in an assertive and nondiscriminatory manner.â

Trump has spent a large part of the last year reviving claims of election fraud, both in his previous electoral losses and in advance of the November midterms, and instructing the Justice Department to pursue an unprecedented litigation campaign against states to purge their voter rolls of supposed noncitizens.

In a July national address, Trump rehashed claims of foreign interference in the 2020 presidential election, asserting China carried out âthe largest compromise of election data in historyâ by acquiring 220 million U.S. voter files.

This past week, Trump urged the Supreme Court to set aside 20,000 ballots from suspected noncitizens in California, marking a major escalation in the administrationâs effort to exert federal control in state-run elections. The Justice Department filed a similar challenge in Washington state, where it advanced further claims of noncitizen voter fraud.

The justices swiftly rejected the request on Monday, denying the governmentâs emergency request before hearing any briefing from California officials in an unsigned order without any noted dissents.

In another unprecedented case, the Justice Department sued to block the certification of election results in Alaskaâs Senate race over concerns voters will be confused by the inclusion of two Dan Sullivans on the ballot. Incumbent Republican Senator Dan Sullivan faces Democrat Representative Mary Peltola, as well as Daniel Sullivan Jr., who is not affiliated with a political party.

On Sunday, U.S. District Judge Sharon Gleason found the Justice Departmentâs request would violate Alaskan votersâ right to a secret ballot.

Throughout the country, the Trump administration has also sought to create a national voter database by demanding states hand over their voter rolls, which it argued would allow it to remove any noncitizens and prevent them from voting Tuesday.

Voting rights organizations sued in Washington, citing several examples of U.S. citizens in red states who complied with such demands having their voter registrations canceled erroneously.

The rolls include sensitive personal data such as home addresses, Social Security numbers and voting history. As a result, while at least 18 states have fully cooperated with the Justice Departmentâs demands, 26 states have refused.

Thatâs led the Justice Department to sue in efforts to compel the states to produce their voter rolls. In all 26 of those states, federal judges have dismissed the Justice Departmentâs lawsuits after ruling the efforts unlawful. Additionally, the Sixth Circuit has also upheld the dismissal of one such lawsuit.

U.S. District Judge Sparkle Sooknanan, a Joe Biden appointee, ruled on Oct. 9 that the Justice Department could not use the flawed database to revoke any U.S. citizenâs voter registration, slamming it as a clear effort to âdecide who gets to vote.â
